Bhāravataraṇa · the compendium

Claimed

Every telling of this part of the story that the ledger holds, in the writings’ own words, laid side by side.

The book retells; this page lets the writings speak. The moments run in the order of the story, as the part’s paragraph in the story on one page tells it (Chapter VIII is not yet written, so the moments and their heads are ours). Inside each moment the writings run from the oldest to the newest, so each moment also shows how its telling came down through time. Every writing has a card: what it is, when it was written, and where its oldest copy sits, when we know. Under the card is every line the ledger holds from it, exactly as the ledger holds it.

536 lines from 242 writings, in 27 moments. A moment marked ✦ is one the one-page story does not tell: the ledger gathered its lines for this part, and they stand where they fall. A line in italics has not yet been checked against the printed book, or is our own translation. There are no ◇ joins yet; they come with the chapter. Click a name for its names in every language.

Moment 1 of 27

Agade asks the gods to make its king a god

The earliest king known to have been declared a god ruled at Agade about 2250 BC. Because Naram-Sin protected the foundations of his city, its citizens asked eight gods, from Aštar in Eanna to Nergal in Kutha, that he be made the god of their city, and they built him a temple within Agade. The Curse of Agade remembers him rising like daylight on the holy throne and then trying to alter Enlil’s word. A century and a half later Šulgi of Ur called himself the god of all the lands, and his own hymn asks what mortal has ever reached the heavens.

Told by 5 writings from 1 people, 9 lines · oldest writing first

The Bassetki statue of Naram-Sin

Mesopotamia · about 2250 BC

A copper statue base found by road builders near Bassetki in northern Iraq in 1974, whose inscription says the city of Agade asked eight gods to make its king Naram-Sin a god and built him a temple.

Oldest copyBaghdad, Iraq Museum

"In view of the fact that he protected the foundations of his city from danger, (the citizens of his city requested from Astar in Eanna, Enlil in Nippur, Dagan in Tuttul, Ninhursag in Kes, Ea in Eridu, Sin in Ur, Samas in Sippar, (and) Nergal in Kutha, that (Naram-Sin) be (made) the god of their city, and they built within Agade a temple (dedicated) to him." {d}na-ra-am-{d}suen / da-num2 / _dingir_ a-ga-de3{ki} / _lugal_ / ki-ib-ra-tim / ar-ba-im (CDLI: 'Naram-Sin, the mighty, the god of Agade, king of the world quarters the four')

The hymns of Šulgi of Ur

Mesopotamia · about 2050 BC

The Sumerian royal hymns of Šulgi, king of Ur, who calls himself god of all the lands and asks what mortal has ever reached the heavens.

"I, the king, was a hero already in the womb; I, Culgi, was born to be a mighty man. I am a fierce-looking lion, begotten by a dragon. I am the king of the four regions; I am the herdsman and shepherd of the black-headed people. I am a respected one, the god of all the lands." (Šulgi A 1-6) "Whatever is acquired is destined to be lost. What mortal has ever reached the heavens?" ... "The academies are never to be altered; the places of learning shall never cease to exist." (Šulgi B 281-296) "The judgment uttered by the assembly cannot be reversed. The word of An and Enlil knows no overturning. Urim was indeed given kingship but it was not given an eternal reign. From time immemorial, since the Land was founded, until people multiplied, who has ever seen a reign of kingship that would take precedence for ever? The reign of its kingship had been long indeed but had to exhaust itself." (Lament for Sumer and Urim 360-370)

F18. Ur: the god of all the lands who knows no mortal reaches heaven, and the assembly that ends his house. · tr. ETCSL · u13-0326

The Curse of Agade

Mesopotamia · about 2000 BC

The Sumerian poem of Naram-Sin, who rose like daylight on the throne of Agade and would change Enlil’s word, and of the city the gods cursed for it.

"Its king, the shepherd Naram-Suen, rose as the daylight on the holy throne of Agade." (40-56) "Naram-Suen persisted for seven years! Who has ever seen a king burying his head in his hands for seven years?" (83-93) "In order to change what had been inflicted (?) upon him, he tried to to alter Enlil's pronouncement." (94-99) [sic, ETCSL] "Like a robber plundering the city, he set tall ladders against the temple." (100-119) "He ripped out its drain pipes, and all the rain went back to the heavens." (120-148) "Enlil, the roaring (?) storm that subjugates the entire land, the rising deluge that cannot be confronted, was considering what should be destroyed in return for the wrecking of his beloved E-kur." (149-175) "If someone decides, "I will dwell in this city!", may he not enjoy the pleasures of a dwelling place!" (256-271) "And before Utu on that very day, so it was!" ... "Inana be praised for the destruction of Agade!" (272-281)

F15. The Curse of Agade: the god-king who would change Enlil's word. · tr. ETCSL · u13-0320

The Sumerian King List

Mesopotamia · copied about 1800 BC

A list of the cities of Sumer and their kings, from before the flood down to the kings of Isin, in Sumerian.

Oldest copyOxford, Ashmolean Museum, AN1923.444, the Weld-Blundell prism (H6)

After the kingship descended from heaven, the kingship was in Eridu.

Mesopotamia · 7th century BC copies of an Old Babylonian text

The tale of the brood the great gods made, birds and men together, and the king of Akkad who fought them.

Oldest copyLondon, British Museum, 1881,0204.219, with Ashurbanipal’s colophon (H36)

"[Open the foundation box and] read well the [s]tela" (SB 1) "Troops with bodies of “cave birds,” humans with raven faces / Did the great gods create, / In the place they created (them), the gods were their cohabitants. / Tiamat suckled them, / Belet-ili their mother made (them) fair. / Inside the mountain(s) they grew up, became adults, got their stature. / Seven kings they were, allies, they were glorious in form, / 6,000 were their troops. / Anu-banini, their father was king; their mother (was named) queen Melili," (31-39) "I set [a dagg]er and a pin in his hand, / “Stab with the d[agg]er, prick with the pin. / “[I]f blood comes out, they are human like us. / “[If] no blood comes out, they are evil fiend, fate, [phan]tom, demons, handiwork of Enlil.”" (64-68) "“I pricked with the pin, and blood came out.”" (71) "“What lion undertook divination? / “What wolf consulted a dream interpreter? / “I will go, as I like, like a brigand, / “I will cast off what belongs to the gods, I will hold fast (only) to myself!”" (80-83)

Moment 2 of 27

Blood, not the ichor of the gods

The claims met their courts. Plutarch’s Alexander, wounded, tells his friends that what flows from him is blood, not the ichor of the gods, and Arrian says his share in divinity did not rest on his mother’s word. Athens hailed Demetrius as a present god, not made of wood or stone, and Herod Agrippa, hailed as a god, said that he whom they called a god was commanded presently to depart this life. The First Emperor of China counted his line forward to ten thousand generations, and the Qurʾān’s Pharaoh proclaims, I am your Lord the Highest.

Told by 9 writings from 6 peoples, 12 lines · oldest writing first

The Athenians’ hymn to Demetrius

Greece · about 291 BC

The hymn the Athenians sang to Demetrius the Besieger as a present god, preserved by Athenaeus, with what Plutarch says the land made of it.

"His friends resemble the bright lesser stars, / Himself is Phœbus. / Hail, ever-mighty Neptune's mightier son; / Hail, son of Venus. / For other gods do at a distance keep, / Or have no ears, / Or no existence; and they heed not us— / But you are present, / Not made of wood or stone, a genuine god. / We pray to thee." (the ithyphallic hymn, Duris in Athenaeus 6.63, Yonge) "the spot where Demetrius first alighted from his chariot they consecrated and covered with an altar, which they styled the altar of Demetrius Alighter" (Plutarch, Demetrius 10.3-4) "Through him it was that hoar-frost blasted all the vines, / Through his impiety the robe was rent in twain, / Because he gave the gods' own honours unto men. / Such work undoes a people, not its comedy." (Philippides, in Plutarch, Demetrius 12.2-4) "Demetrius, after an imprisonment of three years in the Syrian Chersonese, through inactivity and surfeit of food and wine, fell sick and died, in the fifty-fifth year of his life." (52.3)

F21. Demetrius the Besieger: the present god, and what the land said. · tr. Yonge 1854; Perrin (Loeb 1920) · u13-0332

Daniel 8

Hebrew Bible and Enoch · about 165 BC

The vision of the little horn that grew to the host of heaven and cast down some of the stars, read as Antiochus IV, who called himself god manifest.

"And it waxed great, even to the host of heaven; and it cast down some of the host and of the stars to the ground, and stamped upon them." (Daniel 8:10) "And he shall speak great words against the most High, and shall wear out the saints of the most High, and think to change times and laws: and they shall be given into his hand until a time and times and the dividing of time." (Daniel 7:25) "And the man, that thought a little afore he could reach to the stars of heaven, no man could endure to carry for his intolerable stink." (2 Maccabees 9:10) "It is meet to be subject unto God, and that a man that is mortal should not proudly think of himself, as if he were God." (9:12) "Antiochus surnamed Epiphanes gained the name of Epimanes by his conduct." (Polybius 26.1) "They ordained also by a common statute and decree, That every year those days should be kept of the whole nation of the Jews. And this was the end of Antiochus, called Epiphanes." (2 Maccabees 10:8-9)

F22. Antiochus IV "god manifest", the little horn, and the sentence with a timer. · tr. KJV; Paton (Loeb) · u13-0333

Daniel 4

Hebrew Bible and Enoch · about 165 BC

The chapter in which a watcher and a holy one come down from heaven and sentence King Nebuchadnezzar to live as a beast until seven times pass.

"a watcher and an holy one came down from heaven; He cried aloud, and said thus, Hew down the tree" ... "Nevertheless leave the stump of his roots in the earth, even with a band of iron and brass, in the tender grass of the field" ... "Let his heart be changed from man's, and let a beast's heart be given unto him; and let seven times pass over him. This matter is by the decree of the watchers, and the demand by the word of the holy ones: to the intent that the living may know that the most High ruleth in the kingdom of men" (Daniel 4:13-17) "Is not this great Babylon, that I have built for the house of the kingdom by the might of my power, and for the honour of my majesty? While the word was in the king's mouth, there fell a voice from heaven, saying, O king Nebuchadnezzar, to thee it is spoken; The kingdom is departed from thee." (4:30-31) "And at the end of the days I Nebuchadnezzar lifted up mine eyes unto heaven, and mine understanding returned unto me" (4:34) "And the people gave a shout, saying, It is the voice of a god, and not of a man. And immediately the angel of the Lord smote him, because he gave not God the glory: and he was eaten of worms, and gave up the ghost." (Acts 12:22-23) "I, whom you call a god, am commanded presently to depart this life; while Providence thus reproves the lying words you just now said to me; and I, who was by you called immortal, am immediately to be hurried away by death." (Josephus, Ant. 19.8.2, Herod Agrippa I)

F23. The Watchers sentence a king (Daniel 4), and a king hailed a god dies (Acts 12; Josephus). · tr. KJV; Whiston · u13-0335

Sima Qian, Shiji

China · about 90 BC

The Records of the Grand Historian of China, with the line of the fire-officers Chong and Li and the treatise on the heavens.

古有天皇，有地皇，有泰皇，泰皇最貴 (excerpt of the Chinese) 'In antiquity there was the Heavenly Sovereign, the Earthly Sovereign and the Supreme Sovereign; the Supreme Sovereign was the most honoured' (26th year, 221 BCE) 'From now on, abolish posthumous names. We are the First Emperor. Later generations shall be counted, Second, Third, up to ten thousand generations, handed on without end.' (26th year) 「真人者，入水不濡，入火不爇，陵雲氣，與天地久長。」 'The True Man enters water and is not wet, enters fire and is not burned, rides the cloud-vapours, and lasts as long as heaven and earth.' ... 'I long for the True Man: I will call myself "True Man", not zhen.' (35th year) 「有墜星下東郡，至地為石，黔首或刻其石曰「始皇帝死而地分」。」 'A star fell in Dong commandery and became a stone on the ground; someone of the black-haired people carved on it: "The First Emperor dies and the land is divided."' ... 「今年祖龍死。」 'This year the Ancestral Dragon dies.' (36th year) 'I saw the great god in the sea. He said: Are you the envoy of the Western Sovereign? ... What do you seek? I said: the drug that lengthens years and adds to life. The god said: Your king of Qin's rites are meagre; you may see it but not take it.' ... The sea god said: "Bring young men of good name, virgin girls, and the works of the hundred crafts, and you will have it." ... 'Xu Fu found a level plain and broad marshes, stopped there as king, and did not come back.' (Shiji 118)

F26. The First Emperor: the primordial title, ten thousand generations, the True Man, the fallen star. · our rendering · not yet checked against the printed book · u13-0343

Qin was at last destroyed

Texts: Sima Qian, Shiji (c. 90 BCE), chapter 6 ("Qin Shi Huang benji") and chapter 118 (Wu Bei's speech quoting Xu Fu), Chinese text on Wikisource; all English is the sweep's ("ours"). The name is written 徐巿 in Shiji 6 and 徐福 in Shiji 118. Shiji 6 closes … · our rendering · not yet checked against the printed book · u13-0345

'These three spirit mountains are said to lie in the Bohai, not far from men; but when one is about to arrive, a wind draws the boat away. Some, it is said, once reached them: the immortals and the drug of deathlessness are all there; their creatures, birds and beasts, are all white, and their palace gates are of gold and silver. Before one arrives they look like clouds; on arrival the three spirit mountains lie, on the contrary, beneath the water. As one draws near, the wind always pulls one away; in the end no one can reach them. Every ruler of the age longed for them.' 'The Yellow Emperor mounted; ministers and palace women who went up with him were more than seventy; then the dragon rose. The lesser officials who could not get on all clung to the dragon's whiskers; the whiskers came out, they fell, and the Yellow Emperor's bow fell. The people, gazing up after the Yellow Emperor had gone up to heaven, clasped his bow and the whiskers and wailed.' 「嗟乎！吾誠得如黃帝，吾視去妻子如脫屣耳。」 'Ah! If I could truly be like the Yellow Emperor, I would look on leaving wife and children as no more than kicking off a sandal.' (Emperor Wu of Han)

Plutarch, Life of Alexander

Greece · about AD 100

Plutarch’s life of Alexander, with the oracle about the god in Olympias’s bed and the wounded king who says what flows from him is blood, not the ichor of the gods.

"Philip sent Chaeron of Megalopolis to Delphi, by whom an oracle was brought to him from Apollo, who bade him sacrifice to Ammon and hold that god in greatest reverence, but told him he was to lose that one of his eyes which he had applied to the chink in the door when he espied the god, in the form of a serpent, sharing the couch of his wife." (Plutarch, Alexander 3.1-2) "the prophet of Ammon gave him salutation from the god as from a father; whereupon Alexander asked him whether any of the murderers of his father had escaped him. To this the prophet answered by bidding him be guarded in his speech, since his was not a mortal father." (27.5-6) "The king of Egypt who has fled, a mighty man and a warrior, but an old man, will after a time bring a new lord, a young man, mightier and more powerful than he, who will kill him and seize his land; and he shall traverse the world, and shall subjugate all the enemies of Egypt to your service." (Pseudo-Callisthenes, Syriac, Budge p. 3); the Greek in Budge's note (ours): 'The king who fled will come again into Egypt, not ageing but growing young, and will subdue our enemies the Persians' "“Welcome, O Sesonchésis, the youthful god and ruler of the world;”" (Budge p. 43) "This, my friends, that flows here, is blood, and not 'Ichor, such as flows from the veins of the blessed gods.'" (Plutarch, Alexander 28.3) "Forasmuch as Alexander would be a God, let him be a God." (Aelian, Varia Historia 2.19, the Spartans' decree)

F19. Alexander and the last pharaoh: the serpent-god, the oracle, the king who returns young, the wound. · tr. Perrin (Loeb 1919); Budge 1889; Stanley 1665; our rendering (Greek note) · not yet checked against the printed book · u13-0327

Arrian, The Anabasis of Alexander

Greece · about AD 140

The soldier-historian’s account of Alexander’s campaigns, which says his share in divinity did not rest on his mother’s claim.

"Alexander's participation in divinity did not depend on the false assertion of Olympias in regard to the author of his birth, but on what he might report to mankind in his history of the king." (4.10, Callisthenes' boast as reported) "Not even to Heracles himself while still alive were divine honours paid by the Greeks; and even after his death they were withheld until a decree had been published by the oracle of the god at Delphi that men should honour Heracles as a god." (4.11) "At the same time arrived embassies from Greece, the members of which, with crowns upon their own heads, approached Alexander and crowned him with golden crowns, as if forsooth they came to him as special envoys deputed, to pay him divine honours; and his end was not far off." (7.23)

F20. The historian makes the god (Arrian 4.10-11, 7.23). · tr. Chinnock 1884 · u13-0330

The Manʼyōshū

Japan and Korea · 8th century; the rescript 1946

Japan’s oldest anthology of poems, which sings the emperor as a god lodged above the thunder, set beside the imperial rescript of 1 January 1946 that laid the claim down.

皇者 神二四座者 天雲之 雷之上尓 廬為流鴨 (man'yōgana); 大君は神にしませば天雲の雷の上に廬りせるかも (reading); 'Our great lord, being a god, has made his lodging above the thunder of the heavenly clouds' (Man'yōshū 3.235, ours) 'To call him akitsumikami (manifest god) or arahitogami (god appearing as a man) is different from "god" in the sense of an absolute god or an omniscient, omnipotent god: it means that the imperial ancestors appear in the emperor, their divine descendant, and that the emperor is one body with them' (Kokutai no Hongi, 1937, ours) "The ties between me and my people have always been formed by mutual trust and affection. They do not depend upon mere legends or myths. Nor are they predicted [sic] on the false conception that the Emperor is divine, and that the Japanese are superior to other races and destined to rule the world." (Rescript of 1 January 1946, official English)

F28. Japan: the god who lodged above the thunder, and the rescript that laid the claim down. · tr. our rendering; official English (FRUS 1946) · not yet checked against the printed book · u13-0347

Moment 3 of 27

A senate of gods votes him out

Rome made its gods by decree and unmade one by satire. Livy has Romulus hailed as a god, the son of a god, and seen descending from heaven at dawn; Suetonius has Caesar numbered among the gods by formal decree. A star appeared at the games for Caesar, which Augustus declared his father’s, and a former praetor swore he saw Augustus himself go up to heaven. In Seneca’s satire a senate of gods votes Claudius out of heaven, one god complaining that once it was a great thing to become a god, and now they had made it a farce.

Told by 5 writings from 2 peoples, 10 lines · oldest writing first

Rome makes its gods: Livy and Suetonius

Rome · about 27 BC to AD 121

Lines gathered on how Rome made gods of its rulers: Livy on Romulus hailed a god and seen descending from heaven, and Suetonius on Caesar numbered among the gods by decree and a former praetor who swore he saw Augustus going up to heaven.

"the whole of those present hailed Romulus as 'a god, the son of a god, the King and Father of the City of Rome.'" ... "there were some who secretly hinted that he had been torn limb from limb by the senators" ... Proculus Julius: "at break of dawn, to-day, the Father of this City suddenly descended from heaven and appeared to me." (Livy 1.16) "He died in the fifty-sixth year of his age, and was numbered among the gods, not only by a formal decree, but also in the conviction of the common people. For at the first of the games which his heir Augustus gave in honour of his apotheosis, a comet shone for seven successive days, rising about the eleventh hour, and was believed to be the soul of Caesar, who had been taken to heaven; and this is why a star is set upon the crown of his head in his statue." (Suetonius, Divus Iulius 88) "This is what he proclaimed in public, but, in secret, he rejoiced at this auspicious omen, interpreting it as produced for himself" (Pliny, NH 2.23, of Augustus and the comet) "a certain Livius Geminius, a senator, declared on oath, invoking destruction upon himself and his children if he spoke falsely, that he had seen her ascending to heaven and holding converse with the gods; and he called all the other gods and Panthea herself to witness. For this declaration he received a million sesterces." (Dio 59.11.4, of Drusilla)

F24. Rome: divus by decree, comet and sworn witness. · tr. Roberts 1912; Rolfe (Loeb 1914); Bostock-Riley 1855; Cary (Loeb) · u13-0337

swore that he had seen Augustus ascending to heaven after the manner of which tradition tells concerning Proculus and Romulus.

Seneca, The Pumpkinification of Claudius

Rome · AD 54

The satire written at Nero’s court after Claudius died, in which a senate of gods votes the deified emperor out of heaven.

""Once," said he, "it was a great thing to become a god; now you have made it a farce." ... "I propose that from this day forward the godhead be given to none of those who eat the fruits of the earth, or whom mother earth doth nourish."" (9, Janus) "Is it for this I have made peace by land and sea?" ... "God! who will worship this god, who will believe in him? While you make gods of such as he, no one will believe you to be gods." ... "that he be deported from heaven within thirty days, and from Olympus within thirty hours." "This motion was passed without further debate." (10-11, the deified Augustus) "Then Aeacus decreed he should rattle dice for ever in a box with no bottom." (14) "Like as the daystar from on high scatters the stars of night," (4, Apollo on Nero; Latin: Qualis discutiens fugientia Lucifer astra)

Censorinus, On the Birthday

Rome · AD 238

A Latin essay on time, which reports from Varro how the Etruscans measured an age by the longest life and were given ten such ages as a people.

Latin: "Sed ea quod ignorarent homines, portenta mitti divinitus, quibus admonerentur, unumquodque saeculum esse finitum. Haec portenta Etrusci pro haruspicii disciplinaeque suae peritia diligenter observata in libros rettulerunt." Maude: "But in their ignorance of the truth, men have imagined that the gods apprised them by portents of the end of each cycle. Accomplished in the art of the aruspice, the Etruscans after having searched for these portents with attention, enter them in their books." House: "But because men did not know these things, portents were sent by the gods to warn them that each saeculum was finished. These portents the Etruscans, by their skill in haruspicy and their discipline, watched for carefully and entered in their books." (house)

F31. Censorinus 17.5-6: the portents sent at each end, and written down. · tr. Maude 1900 · not yet checked against the printed book · ages-0054

Latin: "Quare in Tuscis historiis, quae octavo eorum saeculo scriptae sunt, ut Varro testatur, et quot numero saecula ei genti data sint, et transactorum singula quanta fuerint quibusve ostentis eorum exitus designati sint, continetur. Itaque scriptum est quattuor prima saecula annorum fuisse centenum, quintum centum viginti trium, sextum undeviginti et centum, septimum totidem, octavum tum demum agi, nonum et decimum superesse, quibus transactis finem fore nominis Etrusci." Maude: "So the Annals of Etruria, written, as Varro tells us, in the Eighth age, mention how many ages are reserved to the nation, how many have passed and by what portents the end of each is signalized. Thus we read that the first four cycles were of 105 years, the fifth of 123, the sixth of 119, as was also the seventh; that the eighth cycle was then begun, and that there remained but the ninth and tenth to run their course, after which the Etruscan name would perish." Maude's note 9: "Annorum fuisse centum et quinque. Lindenbrog has here added the quinque, thus following the edition of Aldus Manucius."

F32. Censorinus 17.6: the ten saecula given to the nation, and the end of the Etruscan name. · tr. Maude 1900 · ages-0056

Latin: "Quoniam igitur civile Romanorum saeculum centum annis transigitur, scire licet in decimo saeculo et primum natalem tuum fuisse et hodiernum esse." House: "Since, then, the civil saeculum of the Romans runs its course in a hundred years, one may know that both your first birthday and today's fall in the tenth saeculum." (house) Latin: "qui libro antiquitatum duodevicensimo ait fuisse Vettium Romae in augurio non ignobilem, ingenio magno, cuivis docto in disceptando parem: eum se audisse dicentem, si ita esset, ut traderent historici de Romuli urbis condendae auguriis ac XII vulturis, quoniam CXX annos incolumis praeterisset populus Romanus, ad mille et ducentos perventurum." Maude: "If things have passed, as reported by the historians, touching the augurs and the twelve vultures who signalized the Foundation of Rome, then the Roman community having passed the term of 120 years full of life, were assured of attaining 1200 years."

F34. Censorinus 17.15: Rome in its tenth saeculum; Vettius and the twelve vultures. · tr. Maude 1900 · not yet checked against the printed book · ages-0058

Servius on Virgil

Rome · late 4th century AD

The great Latin commentary on Virgil, which says a statue of Marsyas stood in free cities as the sign of their freedom.

Latin: "cum Augustus Caesar ludos funebres patri celebraret, die medio stella apparuit. ille eam esse confirmavit parentis sui: unde sunt versus isti compositi." Latin: "ipse animam patris sui esse voluit eique in Capitolio statuam, super caput auream stellam habentem, posuit: inscriptum in basi fuit 'Caesari emitheo'." House: "When Augustus Caesar was holding funeral games for his father, a star appeared at midday. He affirmed that it was his father's; from this these verses were composed." ... "He himself would have it be his father's soul, and set up to him on the Capitol a statue having a golden star above its head; on the base was inscribed 'To Caesar the demigod'." (house)

F38. Servius on Eclogue 9.46-47: the star at Caesar's games, set over his statue. · our rendering · not yet checked against the printed book · ages-0064

Latin: "sed Vulcanius aruspex in contione dixit cometen esse, qui significaret exitum noni saeculi et ingressum decimi; sed quod invitis diis secreta rerum pronuntiaret, statim se esse moriturum: et nondum finita oratione, in ipsa contione concidit. hoc etiam Augustus in libro secundo de memoria vitae suae complexus est" House: "But Vulcanius the haruspex said in the assembly that it was a comet, which signified the going-out of the ninth saeculum and the coming-in of the tenth; but that because he was declaring the secrets of things against the gods' will, he would die at once: and before his speech was finished he fell in that very assembly. Augustus too included this in the second book of his memoir of his life." (house)

↑ the moments

Moment 4 of 27 · ✦ not in the one-page story

Chosen by the god

Other kings said a god had chosen them. The Cyrus Cylinder says the lord of the gods, angry with Babylon, searched all lands for a righteous prince and took Cyrus by the hand, and Isaiah calls the same Cyrus the LORD’s anointed, whose right hand he has held. The Kushite Piye calls himself divine emanation, living image of Atum, and at Meroë the priests could send the king word from the gods that he must die. Ireland gave the choosing to a stone that the ruler must find on the last day of the assembly, and to the Sovereignty, loathsome at first and beautiful at last.

Told by 17 writings from 8 peoples, 30 lines · oldest writing first

Enūma eliš

Mesopotamia · second millennium BC

Babylon’s poem of creation in Akkadian, named for its first words, when on high, and recited at the New Year festival.

Oldest copyBerlin, Vorderasiatisches Museum, VAT 9676, tablet VI from Aššur, and the Nineveh copies in the British Museum, 7th century BC (H7)

"“I shall call [its] name [Babylon], ‘Home of the Great Gods’," (V 129; Akkadian "lubbī-ma šumšu bābi[li] bītāt ilī rabûti") "“Then build Babylon the task as that you requested, / “Let bricks be formed for it, build high the shrine.” / The Anunna-gods set to with hoes, / One (full) year they made its bricks. / When the second year came, / They raised the head of Esagila, the counterpart to Apsu, / They built the lofty ziggurat of Apsu," (VI 57-63) "“(49) NEBERU shall hold the crossing place of heaven and earth, / “They shall not cross above and below without awaiting him, / “Neberu is his star that he made visible in the skies. ... / ‘Neberu (“Crossing”) shall be his name, who holds the middle of it.’ / “He shall fix the motions of the stars of heaven, / “He shall herd all the gods like sheep. / “He shall keep Tiamat subdued, he shall keep her life cut short, / “In the future of humankind, with the passing of time," (VII 124-133) "“Father Enlil has named (him) his own name (50) ‘Lord of the World’.” ... / “His name, like mine, shall be ‘Ea’." (VII 136, 140) "The great gods named his fifty names / “The Fifty,” they made his position supreme. ... / The revelation (of the names) that the “first one” discoursed before him (Marduk), / He wrote down and preserved for the future to hear," (VII 143-158)

The Behistun inscription of Darius I

Iran · about 520 BC

The Persian king’s account, cut high on a cliff, of how Ahuramazda made him king and how the Lie rebelled in the provinces.

Oldest copyBisotun, Kermanshah, on the cliff face

§ IV. (Thus) saith Darius, the king: Eight of my race were kings before (me); I am the ninth. In two lines have we been kings. § V. (Thus) saith Darius, the king: By the grace of Auramazda am I king; Auramazda hath granted me the kingdom. ... § IX. (Thus) saith Darius, the king: Auramazda hath granted unto me this empire. Auramazda brought me help, until I gained this empire; by the grace of Auramazda do I hold this empire. § X. ... When Cambyses had departed into Egypt, the people became hostile, and the lie multiplied in the land, even in Persia, as in Media, and in the other provinces. § XI. (Thus) saith Darius, the king: Afterwards there was a certain man, a Magian, Gaumata by name, who raised a rebellion in Paishiyauvada, in a mountain named Arakadrish. On the fourteenth day of the month Viyakhna did he rebel. He lied unto the people, saying: "I am Smerdis, the son of Cyrus, the brother of Cambyses." ... § LV. (Thus) saith Darius, the king: Thou who mayest be king hereafter, beware of lies; the man who is a liar, destroy him utterly if thou thinkest "(thereby) shall my land remain whole." ... § LXIII. (Thus) saith Darius, the king: On this account Auramazda brought me help, and the other gods, (all) that there are, because I was not wicked, nor was I a liar, nor was I a tyrant, neither I nor any of my line.

Diodorus of Sicily, Library of History

Greece · 1st century BC

A Greek universal history; book 3 tells of the Ethiopians and of the Atlantians by the western ocean.

the priests at Meroë who spend their time in the worship of the gods and the rites which do them honour, being the greatest and most powerful order, whenever the idea comes to them, dispatch a messenger to the king with orders that he die. For the gods, they add, have revealed this to them, and it must be that the command of the immortals should in no wise be disregarded by one of mortal frame.

For assuming a spirit which became the position of a king he entered with his soldiers into the unapproachable place where stood, as it turned out, the golden shrine of the Ethiopians, put the priests to the sword, and after abolishing this custom thereafter ordered affairs after his own will

The Secret History of the Mongols

Tibet, Mongolia and the Steppe · 13th century

The Mongols’ own history of Chinggis Khan’s line, with Alan Qoa and the man of light who came in by the smoke-hole.

"You do not know. Every night a yellowish-white man came in by the bright place of the smoke-hole and the lintel of the door and rubbed my belly. When his light had passed into my belly, he went out along the light of the sun and moon, crawling out like a yellow dog. Do not speak rashly. Seen this way, they are clearly the sons of Heaven (天的兒子) and are not to be compared with ordinary men. Later, when they become emperors and kings, then [men] will know." (Yuanchao bishi, §21)

Moment 5 of 27 · ✦ not in the one-page story

A man on a throne among the gods

The Jewish books know the claim from the inside. A hymn from Qumran has a man speak of a mighty throne in the congregation of the gods, on which none of the ancient kings shall sit, and ask who can be compared with him in his glory; another scroll reads Psalm 82 as the trial of Belial, with Melchizedek judging in the divine assembly. In 3 Enoch the man Enoch is called the Lesser YHWH over the angels’ protests, and when a visitor cried that there are two powers in heaven, he was given sixty lashes of fire. Ezekiel’s prince of Tyre says, I am a god, I sit in the seat of God in the midst of the seas, and is told, yet thou art a man.

Moment 6 of 27 · ✦ not in the one-page story

Horus and Seth before the gods

Egypt put the throne itself on trial: in the Contendings of Horus and Seth, Seth is brought in fetters and the Sun-god takes him to dwell with him as his son, to thunder in the sky and be feared. The Shabaka Stone has Geb think it wrong that the portion of Horus should be like the portion of Seth. A Hyksos king chose Seth for his lord, while Hatshepsut reviled the foreigners who ruled without Re. Israel’s prophet later called Pharaoh the great dragon lying in the midst of his rivers.

Moment 7 of 27 · ✦ not in the one-page story

The council on Olympus

Greece kept the gods’ own court. Pindar tells how Zeus and Poseidon strove for Thetis until Themis told the council she was fated to bear a son mightier than his father, and the Cypria says Zeus swore she should be the wife of a mortal. Thetis had once saved Zeus when Hera, Poseidon and Athena would have bound him, and the Cypria has Zeus plan the Trojan war with Themis. Zeus flung Hephaistos from the threshold of heaven and Ate from the starry sky, swearing she should never return, and an old Hittite rite remembers when the first gods were made the last.

Told by 10 writings from 3 peoples, 19 lines · oldest writing first

A Hittite ritual

Anatolia and the Levant · Hittite, second millennium BC

A ritual text in which, for a priest, the first gods are made the last; the ledger gives it in Elisabeth Rieken’s German, with our English. Its catalogue number is not in the ledger.

"[F]ür den GUDU12-Priester machte man die [ers]ten Götte[r] zu den [let]zten, -- und die [let]zten machte man zu den ersten Göttern" ('for the priest they made the first gods the last, and the last they made the first gods')

tr. Rieken; our rendering · not yet checked against the printed book · u8-0236

Murray 1924 · not yet checked against the printed book · cloud_greece-0264

"Full often have I heard thee glorying in the halls of my father, and declaring that thou alone among the immortals didst ward off shameful ruin from the son of Cronos, lord of the dark clouds, on the day when the other Olympians were fain to put him in bonds, even Hera and Poseidon and Pallas Athene. But thou camest, goddess, and didst loose him from his bonds, when thou hadst with speed called to high Olympus him of the hundred hands, whom the gods call Briareus, but all men Aegaeon; for he is mightier than his father. He sate him down by the side of the son of Cronos, exulting in his glory, and the blessed gods were seized with fear of him, and bound not Zeus." "or I shall take and hurl him into murky Tartarus, far, far away, where is the deepest gulf beneath the earth, the gates whereof are of iron and the threshold of bronze, as far beneath Hades as heaven is above earth: then shall ye know how far the mightiest am I of all gods. Nay, come, make trial, ye gods, that ye all may know. Make ye fast from heaven a chain of gold, and lay ye hold thereof, all ye gods and all goddesses; yet could ye not drag to earth from out of heaven Zeus the counsellor most high, not though ye laboured sore." "no, not though thou shouldst go to the nethermost bounds of earth and sea, where abide Iapetus and Cronos, and have joy neither in the rays of Helios Hyperion nor in any breeze, but deep Tartarus is round about them."

F38. Iliad 1.396-406, 8.13-22, 8.478-481 and 14.278-279: the court that tried to bind Zeus; the hundred-hander with two names; the chain of gold; Iapetus and Cronos below, still sworn by. · tr. Murray 1924; Murray 1925 · cloud_greece-0275

"Yea, on a time ere this, when I was fain to save thee, he caught me by the foot and hurled me from the heavenly threshold; the whole day long was I borne headlong, and at set of sun I fell in Lemnos, and but little life was in me. There did the Sintian folk make haste to tend me for my fall." "she that saved me when pain was come upon me after I had fallen afar through the will of my shameless mother, that was fain to hide me away by reason of my lameness. Then had I suffered woes in heart, had not Eurynome and Thetis received me into their bosom—Eurynome, daughter of backward-flowing Oceanus. With them then for nine years' space I forged much cunning handiwork, brooches, and spiral arm-bands, and rosettes and necklaces, within their hollow cave; and round about me flowed, murmuring with foam, the stream of Oceanus, a flood unspeakable. Neither did any other know thereof, either of gods or of mortal men, but Thetis knew and Eurynome, even they that saved me."

The Cypria

Greece · 7th–6th century BC

A lost epic of the causes of the Trojan War, known from Proclus’s summary and quotations.

Zeus plans with Themis to bring about the Trojan war. Strife arrives while the gods are feasting at the marriage of Peleus and starts a dispute between Hera, Athena, and Aphrodite as to which of them is fairest.

Pindar, Isthmian 8

Greece · 478 BC

Pindar’s ode in which the gods’ council, on Themis’s advice, gives Thetis to a mortal husband.

Thereof was the god's council mindful, what time for the hand of Thetis there was strife between Zeus and glorious Poseidon ... For Themis of wise counsels spake in the midst of them of how it was pre-destined that the sea-goddess should bear a royal offspring mightier than his father, whose hand should wield a bolt more terrible than the lightning or the dread trident, if she came ever into the bed of Zeus, or of brethren of Zeus. / 'Cease ye herefrom: let her enter a mortal's couch and see her son fall in war, who shall be as Ares in the might of his hands, and as the lightning in the swiftness of his feet. ... neither let the daughter of Nereus put a second time into your hands the ballot-leaves of strife.'

Plato, Symposium

Greece · about 385 BC

Plato’s dialogue on love, in which Aristophanes tells of the double men who tried to climb heaven and the gods’ council that hesitated to destroy them.

Terrible was their might and strength, and the thoughts of their hearts were great, and they made an attack upon the gods; of them is told the tale of Otys and Ephialtes who, as Homer says, dared to scale heaven, and would have laid hands upon the gods. Doubt reigned in the celestial councils. Should they kill them and annihilate the race with thunderbolts, as they had done the giants, then there would be an end of the sacrifices and worship which men offered to them; but, on the other hand, the gods could not suffer their insolence to be unrestrained. At last, after a good deal of reflection, Zeus discovered a way. He said: 'Methinks I have a plan which will humble their pride and improve their manners; men shall continue to exist, but I will cut them in two and then they will be diminished in strength and increased in numbers

F55. Symposium 190b-d: the council that hesitates because it needs the sacrifices; the race halved rather than annihilated. · tr. Jowett 1892 · cloud_greece-0335

Plato, Protagoras

Greece · 4th century BC

Plato’s dialogue in which Protagoras tells how Prometheus stole the arts from the workshop of Hephaistos and Athena.

"The appointed hour was approaching when man in his turn was to go forth into the light of day; and Prometheus, not knowing how he could devise his salvation, stole the mechanical arts of Hephaestus and Athene, and fire with them (they could neither have been acquired nor used without fire), and gave them to man. … But Prometheus is said to have been afterwards prosecuted for theft, owing to the blunder of Epimetheus." "Now man, having a share of the divine attributes, was at first the only one of the animals who had any gods, because he alone was of their kindred"

F33. Plato, Protagoras 321c-322a: the arts stolen from the workshop; the theft prosecuted; man the gods' kin. · tr. Jowett 1892 · cloud_greece-0253

Virgil, Aeneid

Rome · about 19 BC

Virgil’s Latin epic of Aeneas the Trojan, who comes to Italy where Saturn once hid.

"Here the ancient sons of Earth, the Titans brood, hurled down by the thunderbolt, writhe in the lowest abyss. Here, too, I saw the twin sons of Aloeus, giant in stature, whose hands essayed to tear down high Heaven and thrust down Jove from his realm above. Salmoneus, too, I saw, who paid a cruel penalty while aping Jove's fires and the thunders of Olympus. He, borne by four horses and brandishing a torch, rode triumphant through the Greek peoples and his city in the heart of Elis, claiming as his own the homage of deity. Madman! to mimic the storm-clouds and inimitable thunder with brass and the tramp of horn-footed horses! But the Father Almighty amid thick clouds launched his bolt—no firebrands he, nor pitch-pines' smoky glare—and drave him headlong with furious whirlwind." "Hic genus antiquum Terrae, Titania pubes, / fulmine deiecti fundo volvuntur in imo." ... "Vidi et crudeles dantem Salmonea poenas, / dum flammas Iovis et sonitus imitatur Olympi. / Quattuor hic invectus equis et lampada quassans"

F11. Aeneid 6.580-594: Tartarus holds the Titans, the twin giants and the man who aped the lightning. · tr. Fairclough 1916 · cloud_troy_lucifer-0217

Ovid, Metamorphoses

Rome · AD 8

Ovid’s Latin poem of changes, in which Jupiter calls the gods to council and lets the lesser gods keep the lands allotted them.

"I was not more troubled than now for the sovereignty of the world when each one of the serpent-footed giants was in act to lay his hundred hands upon the captive sky. For, although that was a savage enemy, their whole attack sprung from one body and one source. But now, wherever old Ocean roars around the earth, I must destroy the race of men. By the infernal streams that glide beneath the earth through Stygian groves, I swear that I have already tried all other means. But that which is incurable must be cut away with the knife, lest the untainted part also draw infection. I have demigods, rustic divinities, nymphs, fauns and satyrs, and sylvan deities upon the mountain-slopes. Since we do not yet esteem them worthy the honour of a place in heaven, let us at least allow them to dwell in safety in the lands allotted them." "cuncta prius temptata: sed inmedicabile corpus / ense recidendum est, ne pars sincera trahatur. / Sunt mihi semidei, sunt rustica numina" (1.190-192) "An infamous report of the age had reached my ears. Eager to prove this false, I descended from high Olympus, and as a god disguised in human form travelled up and down the land. It would take too long to recount how great impiety was found on every hand. The infamous report was far less than the truth." "quam cupiens falsam summo delabor Olympo / et deus humana lustro sub imagine terras." (1.212-213)

F14. Metamorphoses 1.182-195 and 1.211-215: the council, the knife, the demigods; the god who went down in human form to see. · tr. Miller 1916 · cloud_troy_lucifer-0222

"One house has fallen; but not one house alone has deserved to perish. Wherever the plains of earth extend, wild fury reigns supreme. You would deem it a conspiracy of crime. Let them all pay, and quickly too, the penalties which they have deserved. So stands my purpose." When he had done, some proclaimed their approval of his words, and added fuel to his wrath, while others played their parts by giving silent consent. And yet they all grieved over the threatened loss of the human race, and asked what would be the state of the world bereft of mortals. Who would bring incense to their altars? Was he planning to give over the world to the wild beasts to despoil? As they thus questioned, their king bade them be of good cheer (for the rest should be his care), for he would give them another race of wondrous origin far different from the first." "And now he was in act to hurl his thunderbolts 'gainst the whole world; but he stayed his hand in fear lest perchance the sacred heavens should take fire from so huge a conflagration, and burn from pole to pole. He remembered also that 'twas in the fates that a time would come when sea and land ... and the beleaguered structure of the universe should be destroyed by fire. And so he laid aside the bolts which Cyclopean hands had forged. He preferred a different punishment, to destroy the human race beneath the waves and to send down rain from every quarter of the sky." "esse quoque in fatis reminiscitur, adfore tempus, / quo mare, quo tellus correptaque regia caeli / ardeat et mundi moles obsessa laboret." (1.256-258)

F15. Metamorphoses 1.240-261: the verdict; who will bring incense; fire held back because fire is fated; the flood chosen. · tr. Miller 1916 · cloud_troy_lucifer-0223

"Not so all-fostering Earth, who, encircled as she was by sea, amid the waters of the deep, amid her fast-contracting streams which had crowded into her dark bowels and hidden there, though parched by heat, heaved up her smothered face. Raising her shielding hand to her brow and causing all things to shake with her mighty trembling, she sank back a little lower than her wonted place, and then in awful tones she spoke: "If this is thy will, and I have deserved all this, why, O king of all the gods, are thy lightnings idle? If I must die by fire, oh, let me perish by thy fire and lighten my suffering by thought of him who sent it. I scarce can open my lips to speak these words"—the hot smoke was choking her—"See my singed hair and all ashes in my eyes, all ashes over my face. Is this the return, this the reward thou payest of my fertility and dutifulness? that I bear the wounds of the crooked plow and mattock, tormented year in, year out? that I provide kindly pasturage for the flocks, grain for mankind, incense for the altars of the gods? ... If the sea perish and the land and the realms of the sky, then are we hurled back to primeval chaos. Save from the flames whatever yet remains and take thought for the safety of the universe." So spoke the Earth and ceased, for she could no longer endure the heat; and she retreated into herself and into the depths nearer the land of shades." The D-scholion on Iliad 1.5

Greece · ancient commentary; manuscript 10th century

The old Greek note on the Iliad’s “the plan of Zeus”, which says Earth, weighed down by the multitude of men, asked Zeus to lighten her, and quotes the Cypria.

Oldest copyVenice, Biblioteca Marciana, Marc. gr. Z. 454, the Venetus A, 10th century (H16)

Others said that Homer spoke from a certain story. For they say that the earth, weighed down by the multitude of men, there being no piety among men, asked Zeus to be lightened of the load; and Zeus first made the Theban war at once, through which he destroyed very many; and afterwards again he took Momos for his counsellor (this is what Homer calls the plan of Zeus), since he was able to destroy them all with thunderbolts or with floods. Momos prevented this, and put to him two things instead: the marriage of Thetis to a mortal, and the begetting of a beautiful daughter. From these two came war for Greeks and barbarians, and from that time it came about that the earth was lightened, many being done away with. The story is in Stasinus, who made the Cypria, who says thus: (house) "And again the goddess murky Night, though she lay with none, bare Blame and painful Woe"

F18. The scholion's own prose (house): Earth's petition, the impiety, Momos's two proposals; and Hesiod's Momos, Night's fatherless child. · our rendering · not yet checked against the printed book · cloud_greece-0203

Moment 8 of 27 · ✦ not in the one-page story

The old gods driven out

When a new faith came the old gods were put out by name. In Kiev Vladimir had Perun dragged to the river at a horse’s tail and beaten to affront the demon, and the devil groaned that he was driven out by the ignorant, not by apostles. The men of Novgorod told the idol, Little Perun, you have drunk and eaten your fill, now float away, and Herberstein was told it swam against the stream while a voice was heard at the bridge. The Vendidad drives out Indra and the other daevas by name, and in Japan the star-god Kagase-wo alone refused to submit.

Told by 5 writings from 3 peoples, 12 lines · oldest writing first

Kojiki and Nihon shoki

Japan and Korea · 712 and 720

Japan’s first two chronicles, with the Age of the Gods.

"The two Gods at length put to death the malignant Deities and the tribes of herbs, trees and rocks. When all had been subdued, the only one who refused submission was the Star-God Kagase-wo." "Kagase-wo. Wo means male. Kaga is obviously connected with kagayaku, to shine. This is the only Star-God mentioned in Japanese myth, and it may be noted that little honour is shown him. He is described as a conquered rebel, and has neither Kami nor Mikoto affixed to his name. The only stars mentioned in the "Kojiki" or "Nihongi" are Venus, the Pleiades, and the Weaver or Star a Lyrae" "In Heaven there is an Evil Deity called Ama-tsu-mika-hoshi, or Ame no Kagase-wo. We pray that this Deity may be executed before we go down to make clear the Central Land of Reed-Plains."

They also had his hair plucked out, and made him therewith expiate his guilt. … When this was done, they at last banished him downwards." ""I wail because I wish to depart to my deceased mother's land, to the Nether Distant Land." Then the Great August Deity the Male-Who-Invites was very angry and said: "If that be so, thou shalt not dwell in this land," and forthwith expelled him with a divine expulsion."

The 907 treaty: " … " (p. 151). · tr. Cross 1930 · u9-0239

Vladimir then began to reign alone in Kiev, and he set up idols on the hill outside the castle with the hall: one of Perun, made of wood with a head of silver and a mouth of gold, and others of Khors, Dazhbog, Stribog, Simargl, and Mokosh. The people sacrificed to them, calling them gods, and brought their sons and their daughters to sacrifice them to these devils. They desecrated the earth with their offerings, and the land of Rus and this hill were defiled with blood. But our gracious God desires not the death of sinners, and upon this hill now stands a church dedicated to St Basil, as we shall later narrate.

He thus ordered that Perun should be bound to a horse's tail and dragged along Borichev to the river. Great art thou, oh Lord, and marvelous are thy works! Yesterday he was honored of men, but today held in derision.

Nor did this people know God, but I rejoiced in the service they rendered unto me. But now I am vanquished by the ignorant, not by apostles and martyrs, and my reign in these regions is at an end.'

Moment 9 of 27

I am God and there is no other

In Isaiah the LORD says there is no god beside him. The Gnostic books put the line in the mouth of the blind maker of this world, I am God and there is no other God beside me, and have his mother answer, Do not lie, Ialdabaoth. The Russian chronicle’s Sathanael says he will be like God and set his throne upon the northern clouds, and in the Life of Adam and Eve the elder angel blames Adam for his expulsion from the glory he had in the heavens. The Yezidi Book of Revelation speaks in the angel’s own voice: I was, am now, and shall have no end.

The Life of Adam and Eve

Hebrew Bible and Enoch · first centuries AD

A book outside the Bible, surviving in Latin and Greek, of Adam and Eve after Eden, in which the devil tells why he fell.

"O Adam! all my hostility, envy, and sorrow is for thee, since it is for thee that I have been expelled from my glory, which I possessed in the heavens in the midst of the angels and for thee was I cast out in the earth." (Vita 12.1-2) "And Michael went out and called all the angels saying: 'Worship the image of God as the Lord God hath commanded.' And Michael himself worshipped first; then he called me and said: 'Worship the image of God the Lord.' And I answered, 'I have no (need) to worship Adam.' And since Michael kept urging me to worship, I said to him, 'Why dost thou urge me? I will not worship an inferior and younger being (than I). I am his senior in the Creation, before he was made was I already made. It is his duty to worship me.'" (14.1-3) "When the angels, who were under me, heard this, they refused to worship him. And Michael saith, 'Worship the image of God, but if thou wilt not worship him, the Lord God will be wrath with thee.' And I said, 'If He be wrath with me, I will set my seat above the stars of heaven and will be like the Highest.'" (15.1-3) "And with guile I cheated thy wife and caused thee to be expelled through her (doing) from thy joy and luxury, as I have been driven out of my glory." (16.4) "Rise up and we will cause him to be cast out of paradise, even as we were cast out through him." (Apocalypse of Moses 16:3, the devil to the serpent) "Fear not, for as soon as thou eatest of it, ye too shall be as God, in that ye shall know good and evil. But God perceived this that ye would be like Him, so he envied you and said, Ye shall not eat of it." (18:3-5, the serpent to Eve) "By the throne of the Master, and by the Cherubim and the Tree of Life! I will give also to my husband to eat." (19:2-3, Eve's oath to the serpent)

The Bundahishn

Iran · 9th–10th century AD

The Creation, a Middle Persian book put together from older Zoroastrian teaching.

Oldest copyCopenhagen, Royal Library, K20 (the Indian Bundahišn), about 1351–1400 (H32)

3. ... that wicked evil spirit, through fear of the righteous man, was not able to lift up his head until the wicked Geh came, at the completion of the three thousand years. 4. And she shouted to the evil spirit thus: 'Rise up, thou father of us! for I will cause that conflict in the world wherefrom the distress and injury of Auharmazd and the archangels will arise.' ... 7. ... the evil spirit was delighted and started up from that confusion; and he kissed Geh upon the head, and the pollution which they call menstruation became apparent in Geh. 8. He shouted to Geh thus: 'What is thy wish? so that I may give it thee.' And Geh shouted to the evil spirit thus: 'A man is the wish, so give it to me.' 9. The form of the evil spirit was a log-like lizard's (vazak) body, and he appeared a young man of fifteen years to Geh, and that brought the thoughts of Geh to him. 10. Afterwards, the evil spirit, with the confederate demons, went towards the luminaries, and he saw the sky; and he led them up, fraught with malicious intentions. 11. He stood upon one-third of the inside of the sky, and he sprang, like a snake, out of the sky down to the earth. 12. In the month Fravardin and the day Auharmazd he rushed in at noon, and thereby the sky was as shattered and frightened by him, as a sheep by a wolf. ... 15. And noxious creatures were diffused by him over the earth, biting and venomous, such as the snake, scorpion, frog (kalvak), and lizard (vazak), so that not so much as the point of a needle remained free from noxious creatures. ... 26. And ninety days and nights the heavenly angels were contending in the world with the confederate demons of the evil spirit, and hurled them confounded to hell; and the rampart of the sky was formed so that the adversary should not be able to mingle with it.

↑ the moments

Moment 10 of 27

How art thou fallen, O Lucifer

The name comes from Isaiah’s song over a king of Babylon, told as the fall of a star: in the Hebrew he is Helel son of Dawn, who said he would sit on the mount of meeting in the far recesses of Zaphon and is brought down to Sheol. The Greek Bible makes him the dawn-bringer who rose early and the Latin makes him Lucifer; the King James Bible prints the name with a capital, once. Ugarit knew the same mountain, where Athtar went up to Baal’s throne on Zaphon and his feet did not reach the footstool, and Ezekiel’s king of Tyre was a cherub in Eden, every precious stone his covering.

Told by 12 writings from 5 peoples, 27 lines · oldest writing first

The Baal cycle

Anatolia and the Levant · 14th–13th century BC

The Ugaritic poems of the storm-god Baal, on six clay tablets from Ugarit (KTU 1.1–1.6): Anat’s boast of the serpent she struck down, and ʿAthtar, too small for Baal’s throne, going down to rule the earth.

Oldest copytablet 6: Paris, Louvre, AO 16636 (H25)

Then Athtar ʿrẓ went up into the recesses of Zaphon; he sat on the throne of Mightiest Baal. His feet did not reach the footstool; his head did not reach its top. And Athtar ʿrẓ said: I cannot (or: I will not) be king in the recesses of Zaphon. Athtar ʿrẓ came down, came down from the throne of Mightiest Baal, and became king in the earth, god (or: El's earth) of all of it.

Zechariah 11, with Jerome’s commentary

Hebrew Bible and Enoch · Persian period; Jerome about AD 406

The prophet’s cry, Howl, fir tree, for the cedar is fallen, whose Hebrew word for howl is spelled like Isaiah’s Helel; Jerome read it as a command.

"הֵילֵ֤ל בְּרוֹשׁ֙ כִּֽי־נָ֣פַל אֶ֔רֶז ... כִּ֥י יָרַ֖ד יַ֥עַר הבצור׃" "11:2 Howl, fir tree; for the cedar is fallen; because the mighty are spoiled: howl, O ye oaks of Bashan; for the forest of the vintage is come down." "[11:2] Ulula, abies, quia cecidit cedrus ..." Jerome: "Pro eo quod nos interpretati sumus ob facilitatem intelligentiae, Quomodo cecidisti de coelo, lucifer, qui mane oriebaris, in Hebraico, ut verbum exprimamus ad verbum, legitur, Quomodo cecidisti de coelo, ulula fili diluculi. Significatur autem aliis verbis lucifer; et dicitur ei quod flere debeat et lugere, qui quondam sic [erat] gloriosus, ut fulgori luciferi comparatus sit." (house) "Where we, for ease of understanding, translated "How you fell from heaven, lucifer, who rose at morning," the Hebrew, to render it word for word, reads: "How you fell from heaven: howl, son of the dawn." Lucifer is meant in other words; and he is told that he must weep and mourn, he who was once so glorious that he was compared to the brightness of the morning star."

1 Enoch

Hebrew Bible and Enoch · 3rd century BC to 1st century AD

Five Jewish books under the name of Enoch. The oldest, the Book of the Watchers (chapters 1–36), tells of the two hundred who came down on Hermon; the Parables (37–71) are the latest. Known in Aramaic from Qumran and whole only in Geʽez, the old language of Ethiopia.

The Latin and Greek dictionaries on Lucifer

Europe, later writers · 1879–1912

One ledger line from the standard dictionaries, Lewis and Short’s Latin and Liddell and Scott’s Greek, with a plain astronomy of 1912, on Lucifer as the morning star.

"Lūcĭfer, fĕri, m. The morning-star, the planet Venus: stella Lucifer interdiu, noctu Hesperus ita circumeunt, Varr. R. R. 3, 5, 17 … The fabled son of Aurora and Cephalus, and father of Ceyx, Hyg. Astr. 2, 42; Ov. M. 11, 271; 346; acc. to others, a son of Jupiter, Serv. Verg. A. 4, 130.— Poet. transf., day" "φωσφόρος … I. bringing or giving light, Eur., Ar.:—as Subst., ὁ φωσφόρος (sc. ἀστήρ), the lightbringer, Lat. Lucifer, i. e. the morning-star, a name specially given to the planet Venus, Cic. … II. torch-bearing, epith. of certain deities, esp. of Hecate, Eur." "ἕσπερος … I. of or at evening, ἕ. ἀστήρ the evening star, Il.; as Subst., without ἀστήρ, Hesperus, esp. of the planet Venus, Eur., Bion.; but, ἕσπ. θεός the god of darkness, i. e. Hades, death, Soph." "Venus, for instance, they called Phosphorus, or Lucifer, when they saw her as a morning star, and Hesperus, or Vesper, when she shone in the evening." "But an inferior planet is not, like the superior planets and the stars, brightest when it is nearest to us. It is, in fact, darkest when it is nearest--that is, when it is at inferior conjunction--and we cannot see it at all." "During this capricious sort of progress the planet usually describes more or less of a loop, sometimes almost a flourish, in its path."

The Catholic Encyclopedia, “Lucifer”

Christian, Gnostic and Hermetic · 1910

A. J. Maas’s article, which says that Lucifer first named the planet Venus and is not a proper name.

"The name Lucifer originally denotes the planet Venus, emphasizing its brilliance. ... The Syriac version and the version of Aquila derive the Hebrew noun helel from the verb yalal, "to lament"; St. Jerome agrees with them (In Isaiah 1:14), and makes Lucifer the name of the principal fallen angel who must lament the loss of his original glory bright as the morning star. In Christian tradition this meaning of Lucifer has prevailed; the Fathers maintain that Lucifer is not the proper name of the devil, but denotes only the state from which he has fallen (Petavius, De Angelis, III, iii, 4)."

F57. The Catholic Encyclopedia, "Lucifer" (Maas 1910): not a proper name. · cloud_troy_lucifer-0354

Moment 11 of 27 · ✦ not in the one-page story

The morning star in the church

The church kept both meanings of the word: the Vulgate’s lucifer is the morning star itself in the Psalm and in Job, Second Peter waits for the day-star to rise in the heart, Revelation’s Jesus is the bright and morning star, and the Easter Exsultet hails the lucifer which knows no setting. Origen reads Isaiah as the fall of one who was once Lucifer and arose in the morning, and Tertullian calls him, before he became the devil, the wisest of creatures. Jerome found the Saracens of Elusa worshipping Venus for the sake of Lucifer, and a bishop named Lucifer gave his name to a party of the church. Dante climbs out of hell along the body of the creature who once was fair, and sees the planet of love making the whole east laugh.

Told by 12 writings from 3 peoples, 16 lines · oldest writing first

Tertullian, Against Marcion

Christian, Gnostic and Hermetic · about 207–208

The North African theologian’s attack on Marcion, which calls the devil, before his fall, the wisest of creatures.

"Indeed, before he became the devil, he stands forth the wisest of creatures; and wisdom is no evil. ... nor placed with a cherub upon God's holy mountain, that is to say, in the heights of heaven, from which the Lord testifies that Satan fell; nor detained amongst the stones of fire, and the flashing rays of burning constellations, whence Satan was cast down like lightning." "This must mean the devil, whom in another passage (since such will they there have the apostle's meaning to be) we shall recognize in the appellation the god of this world. For he has filled the whole world with the lying pretence of his own divinity." 5.11, of the devil, "who once said, as the prophet describes him: 'I will be like the Most High; I will exalt my throne in the clouds'"

F56. Tertullian, Against Marcion 2.10, 5.17 (and 5.11): the wisest of creatures; the god of this world. · tr. Holmes 1868 · cloud_troy_lucifer-0353

Origen, On First Principles

Christian, Gnostic and Hermetic · about 220–230

The Alexandrian theologian’s system of doctrine, which reads Isaiah’s fallen star and Luke’s falling lightning together.

"Most evidently by these words is he shown to have fallen from heaven, who formerly was Lucifer, and who used to arise in the morning. For if, as some think, he was a nature of darkness, how is Lucifer said to have existed before? Or how could he arise in the morning, who had in himself nothing of the light? Nay, even the Saviour Himself teaches us, saying of the devil, "Behold, I see Satan fallen from heaven like lightning." For at one time he was light." Origen's text of Isa 14:21: "Prepare thy sons for death on account of the sins of thy father, lest they rise again and inherit the earth, and fill the earth with wars." "We are of opinion, therefore, that these words are spoken of a certain angel who had received the office of governing the nation of the Tyrians, and to whom also their souls had been entrusted to be taken care of."

F55. Origen, De Principiis 1.5.5 and 1.5.4: once light; the angel of a nation. · tr. Crombie 1869 · cloud_troy_lucifer-0351

Jerome, Dialogue against the Luciferians

Christian, Gnostic and Hermetic · about 379

Jerome’s dialogue against the followers of Lucifer, bishop of Cagliari in Sardinia, a man named for the morning star.

"This Dialogue was written about 379, seven years after the death of Lucifer ... Though he received ordination from Paulinus, who had been consecrated by Lucifer, he had no sympathy with Lucifer's narrower views ... the tone and temper of which is indicated by the mere titles De Regibus Apostaticis (of Apostate Kings), De non Conveniendo cum Hæreticis, etc. (of not holding communion with heretics)." "The whole world groaned, and was astonished to find itself Arian." "L. ... I must, however, make a confession, because I best know the character of my party, and own that they are more easily conquered than convinced." "In the course of this exile Lucifer wrote an extremely virulent pamphlet ... Lucifer boasted of his work, and Constantius, tyrant that he was, refrained from further revenge."

F60. Jerome's Dialogue against the Luciferians, with Leclercq: the bishop named Lucifer; the world that groaned. · tr. Fremantle 1893; Leclercq 1910 · cloud_troy_lucifer-0360

The letters and saints’ lives of the translator of the Latin Bible, which speak of Lucifer fallen from heaven and of the Saracens of Elusa keeping the festival of the morning star.

"In the West the Sun of righteousness is even now rising; in the East, Lucifer, who fell from heaven, has once more set his throne above the stars." The Exsultet

The Easter proclamation of the Latin church, sung over the paschal candle; it ends on the morning star that knows no setting.

May the morning star find its flame still burning: that morning star, I say, which knows no setting; he who, come back from the dead, shone serene upon the human race."

Moment 12 of 27 · ✦ not in the one-page story

Dawn’s son in the poets

Hesiod gives the star its birth: Dawn bore Eosphoros, the dawn-bringer, to Astraeus, son of a Titan. Homer sets the evening star, fairest in heaven, on Achilles’ spear, and Plato’s epigram on Aster has the boy shine as the morning star among the living and as Hesperus among the dead. Virgil’s Morning Star is the one Venus loves above all the stellar fires, while Ovid’s Lucifer fathers the peaceful king Ceyx. In China the poet Li Bai was named for the evening star, Changgeng, which entered his mother’s dream on the night of his birth.

Told by 13 writings from 4 peoples, 30 lines · oldest writing first

Homer, Iliad

Greece · 8th–7th century BC

The epic of the war at Troy, sung in the Greek cities of Asia Minor.

Oldest copyVenice, Biblioteca Marciana, Marc. gr. Z. 454, the Venetus A, 10th century (H16)

ἕσπερος, ὃς κάλλιστος ἐν οὐρανῷ ἵσταται ἀστήρ, "As a star goeth forth amid stars in the darkness of night, the star of evening, that is set in heaven as the fairest of all; even so went forth a gleam from the keen spear that Achilles poised in his right hand," ἦμος δ' ἑωσφόρος εἶσι φόως ἐρέων ἐπὶ γαῖαν, "But at the hour when the star of morning goeth forth to herald light over the face of the earth—the star after which followeth saffron-robed Dawn and spreadeth over the sea—even then grew the burning faint, and the flame thereof died down."

F55. Homer: the evening star on Achilles' spear; the morning star over Patroclus's pyre. · tr. original (Greek); Murray (Loeb 1924) · cloud_east_venus-0300

"Now Dawn rose from her couch from beside lordly Tithonus, to bring light to immortals and to mortal men; and Zeus sent forth Strife unto the swift ships of the Achaeans, dread Strife, bearing in her hands a portent of war." "As a star goeth forth amid stars in the darkness of night, the star of evening, that is set in heaven as the fairest of all; even so went forth a gleam from the keen spear that Achilles poised in his right hand, as he devised evil for goodly Hector" "But at the hour when the star of morning goeth forth to herald light over the face of the earth—the star after which followeth saffron-robed Dawn and spreadeth over the sea—even then grew the burning faint, and the flame thereof died down."

Hesiod, Theogony

Greece · about 700 BC

The Birth of the Gods, by Hesiod, a poet of Boeotia in central Greece.

τοὺς δὲ μέτ' ἀστέρα τίκτεν Ἑωσφόρον Ἠριγένεια "And after these Erigenia bare the star Eosphorus (Dawn-bringer), and the gleaming stars with which heaven is crowned."

F56. Hesiod, Theogony 381: the star's birth. · tr. original (Greek); Evelyn-White (Loeb 1914) · cloud_east_venus-0306

And Eos bare to Astraeus the strong-hearted winds, brightening Zephyrus, and Boreas, headlong in his course, and Notus,—a goddess mating in love with a god. And after these Erigenia bare the star Eosphorus (Dawn-bringer), and the gleaming stars with which heaven is crowned.

F4. Theogony 378-382: the morning star's only Hesiodic line. · tr. Evelyn-White 1914 · cloud_greece-0160

(ll. 375-377) And Eurybia, bright goddess, was joined in love to Crius and bare great Astraeus ... (ll. 378-382) And Eos bare to Astraeus the strong-hearted winds ... And after these Erigenia bare the star Eosphorus (Dawn-bringer), and the gleaming stars with which heaven is crowned.

Shijing, the Book of Odes

China · about 1000–600 BC

The oldest Chinese book of poems; the ode Da dong names the star of the east Qiming and the star of the west Changgeng, which James Legge put into English as Lucifer and Hesperus.

東有啟明，西有長庚 "In the east there is Lucifer; In the west there is Hesperus;" "Liu I (Sung dynasty) says: 'The metal star (Venus) is in the east in the morning, thus "opening the brightness of the day;" and it is in the west in the evening, thus "prolonging the day."' The author of the piece, however, evidently took Lucifer and Hesperus to be two stars."

F20. Shijing, Da dong (Mao 203), and Legge's note: two names, one star, and the translator who wrote Lucifer. · tr. original (Chinese); Legge 1871; Legge (SBE 3, 1879) · cloud_east_venus-0161

Plato, Timaeus

Greece · 4th century BC

Plato’s dialogue on the making of the world, which opens with the story of Atlantis that Egyptian priests told Solon.

Lucifer, or Hesperus (which is the same)

F45. Timaeus 38d and Epinomis 987b-e: the morning star given the contrary power; " … "; the naming called Syrian. · tr. Lamb 1927 · cloud_greece-0298

"First, there was the moon in the orbit nearest the earth, and next the sun, in the second orbit above the earth; then came the morning star and the star sacred to Hermes, moving in orbits which have an equal swiftness with the sun, but in an opposite direction; and this is the reason why the sun and Hermes and Lucifer overtake and are overtaken by each other." "For indeed they have received titles of gods: thus, that Lucifer, or Hesperus (which is the same), should almost belong to Aphrodite, is reasonable, and quite befitting a Syrian lawgiver; and that that which follows the same course as the sun and this together should almost belong to Hermes. … And let us note that whatever Greeks acquire from foreigners is finally turned by them into something nobler"

Sima Qian, Shiji

China · about 90 BC

The Records of the Grand Historian of China, with the line of the fire-officers Chong and Li and the treatise on the heavens.

察日行以處位太白。曰西方，秋……日庚、辛，主殺。殺失者，罰出太白。太白失行，以其捨命國。其出行十八舍二百四十日而入。入東方，伏行十一舍百三十日；其入西方，伏行三舍十六日而出。當出不出，當入不入，是謂失舍，不有破軍，必有國君之篡。 "Watch the sun's course to place Taibai. It is the west, autumn … the days geng and xin; it governs killing. When killing goes wrong, the punishment comes out of Taibai. When Taibai loses its course, the lodge it occupies dooms a state. It comes out and travels eighteen lodges, 240 days, then goes in. Going in in the east, it travels hidden eleven lodges, 130 days; going in in the west, it travels hidden three lodges, sixteen days, then comes out. When it should come out and does not, should go in and does not, that is called losing its lodge: if there is no broken army there is a usurpation of the ruler." (house) 太白，大臣也，其號上公。其他名殷星、太正、營星、觀星、宮星、明星、大衰、大澤、終星、大相、天浩、序星、月緯。大司馬位謹候此。 "Taibai is the great minister; its title is Supreme Duke. Its other names: Yin Star, Great Regulator, Camp Star, Watching Star, Palace Star, Bright Star, Great Decline, Great Marsh, Final Star, Great Minister, Heaven's Vastness, Order Star, Moon's Woof. The Grand Marshal's office watches it with care." (house) 用兵象太白……太白伏也，以出兵，兵有殃。 "In using troops take Taibai as the model … When Taibai is hidden, to send out troops brings disaster on them." (house)

F21. Shiji 27, Tianguan shu: the Grand White, a general with thirteen other names, and the days of killing. · our rendering · not yet checked against the printed book · cloud_east_venus-0165

Cicero, On the Nature of the Gods

Rome · 45 BC

Cicero’s dialogue on the gods, which says the star of Venus is Phosphoros in Greek and Lucifer in Latin when it goes before the sun, and Hesperos when it follows.

Infima est quinque errantium terraeque proxuma stella Veneris, quae Φωσφόρος Graece Lucifer Latine dicitur cum antegreditur solem, cum subsequitur autem Ἕσπερος; ea cursum anno conficit et latitudinem lustrans signiferi orbis et longitudinem, quod idem faciunt stellae superiores, neque umquam ab sole duorum signorum intervallo longius discedit tum antecedens tum subsequens. "The lowest of the five planets, and nearest the earth, is that of Venus (called in Greek Phosphoros). Before the rising of the sun, it is called the morning-star, and after the setting, the evening-star. It has the same revolution through the zodiac, both as to latitude and longitude, with the other planets, in a year, and never is more than two signs from the sun, whether it precedes or follows it."

F60. Cicero, De Natura Deorum 2.53: Phosphoros in Greek, Lucifer in Latin, Hesperos after the Sun; the leash of two signs. · tr. original (Latin); Yonge 1877 · cloud_east_venus-0319

Virgil, Aeneid

Rome · about 19 BC

Virgil’s Latin epic of Aeneas the Trojan, who comes to Italy where Saturn once hid.

"And now above Ida's topmost ridges the day-star was rising, ushering in the morn; and the Danaans held the blockaded gates, nor was any hope of help offered. I gave way and, taking up my father, sought the mountains." "Iamque iugis summae surgebat Lucifer Idae / ducebatque diem, Danaique obsessa tenebant / limina portarum, nec spes opis ulla dabatur; / cessi, et sublato montes genitore petivi."

F4. Aeneid 2.801-804: the day-star over Ida. · tr. Fairclough 1916 · cloud_troy_lucifer-0205

"With twice ten ships I climbed the Phrygian sea, following the fates declared, my goddess-mother pointing me the way;" Servius: "MATRE DEA MONSTRANTE VIAM hoc loco per transitum tangit historiam, quam per legem artis poeticae aperte non potest ponere. nam Varro in secundo divinarum dicit ex quo de Troia est egressus Aeneas, Veneris eum per diem cotidie stellam vidisse, donec ad agrum Laurentem veniret, in quo eam non vidit ulterius: qua re terras cognovit esse fatales ... quod autem diximus eum poetica arte prohiberi, ne aperte ponat historiam, certum est. Lucanus namque ideo in numero poetarum esse non meruit, quia videtur historiam composuisse, non poema." (house) "MY GODDESS-MOTHER POINTING THE WAY: here in passing he touches a history that the law of the poet's art does not let him set down openly. For Varro, in the second book of the *Divine Antiquities*, says that from the time Aeneas left Troy he saw the star of Venus by day, every day, until he came to the Laurentine field, where he saw it no more; and by this he knew these were the fated lands. ... That the poet's art forbids him to set down history openly is certain: Lucan did not earn a place among the poets for this reason, that he seems to have written a history, not a poem."

F5. Aeneid 1.381-382 with Servius: Varro's star seen every day until the fated field, and the law that hides history in a poem. · tr. Fairclough 1916; our rendering (Servius) · not yet checked against the printed book · cloud_troy_lucifer-0207

"even as the Morning Star, whom Venus loves above all the stellar fires, when, bathed in Ocean's wave, he uplifts in heaven his sacred head and melts the darkness." "qualis ubi Oceani perfusus Lucifer unda, / quem Venus ante alios astrorum diligit ignis, / extulit os sacrum caelo tenebrasque resolvit."

F6. Aeneid 8.589-591: the Morning Star whom Venus loves. · tr. Fairclough 1918 · cloud_troy_lucifer-0209
