Ovid, Metamorphoses

Rome · AD 8

Ovid’s Latin poem of changes, in which Jupiter calls the gods to council and lets the lesser gods keep the lands allotted them.

"Now while the ambitious Phaethon is gazing in wonder at the workmanship, behold, Aurora, who keeps watch in the reddening dawn, has opened wide her purple gates, and her courts glowing with rosy light. The stars all flee away, and the morning star closes their ranks as, last of all, he departs from his watch-tower in the sky." "Diffugiunt stellae, quarum agmina cogit / Lucifer et caeli statione novissimus exit." "Dim and unrecognizable was Lucifer that dawn; and since he might not leave his station in the skies, he wrapped his face in thick clouds." "Lucifer obscurus nec quem cognoscere posses / illa luce fuit, quoniamque excedere caelo / non licuit, densis texit sua nubibus ora." Lewis and Short, *cōgo*: "Agmen, milit. t. t., to bring up the rear (cf. claudo, I. B. 2.)".

F17. Metamorphoses 2.111-115 and 11.570-572: the morning star closes the ranks and leaves last; the star who could not leave his post. · tr. Miller 1916; Lewis and Short 1879 · cloud_troy_lucifer-0230

"As Lucifer shines more brightly than all the other stars and as the golden moon outshines Lucifer, so much was Herse more lovely than all the maidens round her," "Quanto splendidior quam cetera sidera fulget / Lucifer, et quanto quam Lucifer aurea Phoebe" "the morning-star was of dusky hue and his face was blotched with dark red spots, and Luna's chariot was stained with blood." "Caerulus et vultum ferrugine Lucifer atra / sparsus erat" (15.789-790) "Scarce had he spoken when fostering Venus took her place within the senate-house, unseen of all, caught up the passing soul of her Caesar from his body, and not suffering it to vanish into air, she bore it towards the stars of heaven. And as she bore it she felt it glow and burn, and released it from her bosom. Higher than the moon it mounted up and, leaving behind it a long fiery train, gleamed as a star."

F20. Metamorphoses 2.722-725, 15.789-790 and 15.843-850: Lucifer outshone by the Moon; the star blotched at Caesar's murder; Venus carries a soul up to be a star. · tr. Miller 1916 · cloud_troy_lucifer-0237

"Here ruled in peaceful, bloodless sway Ceyx, son of Lucifer, with all his father's bright gladness in his face. But at that time he was sad and unlike himself, for he was mourning the taking off of his brother." "He was once a man (and, so fixed is character, his only qualities were harshness, eagerness for war, readiness for violence), by name Daedalion. We two were born of that god who wakes the dawn and passes last from the sky. I was by nature peaceful and my care was always for preserving peace and for my wife. But cruel war was my brother's pleasure. His fierce courage subdued kings and nations, and now in changed form it pursues the doves of Thisbe." "Lucifero genitore satus patriumque nitorem / ore ferens Ceyx" (11.271-272); "qui vocat auroram caeloque novissimus exit" (11.296) Lewis and Short, *Lūcĭfer*: "The morning-star, the planet Venus ... The fabled son of Aurora and Cephalus, and father of Ceyx, Hyg. Astr. 2, 42; Ov. M. 11, 271; 346; acc. to others, a son of Jupiter, Serv. Verg. A. 4, 130."

Pliny, Natural History

Rome · AD 77

The Roman encyclopedia of Pliny the Elder, who died at Vesuvius in 79: book 2 on the stars, book 5 on the coast of Phoenicia.

"Below the Sun revolves the great star called Venus, wandering with an alternate motion, and, even in its surnames, rivalling the Sun and the Moon. For when it precedes the day and rises in the morning, it receives the name of Lucifer, as if it were another sun, hastening on the day. On the contrary, when it shines in the west, it is named Vesper, as prolonging the light, and performing the office of the moon. Pythagoras, the Samian, was the first who discovered its nature, about the 62nd olympiad, in the 222nd year of the City. It excels all the other stars in size, and its brilliancy is so considerable, that it is the only star which produces a shadow by its rays. There has, consequently, been great interest made for its name; some have called it the star of Juno, others of Isis, and others of the Mother of the Gods. By its influence everything in the earth is generated. For, as it rises in either direction, it sprinkles everything with its genial dew, and not only matures the productions of the earth, but stimulates all living things. It completes the circuit of the zodiac in 348 days, never receding from the sun more than 46 degrees, according to Timæus." Infra solem ambit ingens sidus appellatum Veneris, alterno meatu vagum ipsisque cognominibus aemulum solis ac lunae. praeveniens quippe et ante matutinum exoriens luciferi nomen accepit ut sol alter diemque maturans, contra ab occasu refulgens nuncupatur vesper ut prorogans lucem vicemque lunae reddens. quam naturam eius Pythagoras Samius primus deprehendit Olympiade circiter XLII, qui fuit urbis Romae annus CXLII. iam magnitudine extra cuncta alia sidera est, claritatis quidem tantae, ut unius huius stellae radiis umbrae reddantur.

F59. Pliny, Natural History 2.36-37: the star's two surnames; the first discoverer; the dew. · tr. Bostock and Riley 1855; original (Latin) · cloud_east_venus-0315

"For when it precedes the day and rises in the morning, it receives the name of Lucifer, as if it were another sun, hastening on the day. On the contrary, when it shines in the west, it is named Vesper, as prolonging the light, and performing the office of the moon. Pythagoras, the Samian, was the first who discovered its nature" Latin: "luciferi nomen accepit ut sol alter diemque maturans" Lewis and Short, *lūcĭfer*: "adj. lux-fero, light-bringing: itaque ut apud Graecos Dianam, eamque Luciferam, sic apud nostros Junonem Lucinam in pariendo invocant, Cic. N. D. 2, 27, 68 ... Subst.: Lūcĭfer, fĕri, m. The morning-star, the planet Venus"

Diogenes Laertius, Lives of the Philosophers

Greece · 3rd century AD

A Greek compilation of philosophers’ lives whose prologue reports what the Greeks said of the Magi.

"XIV. Parmenides, too, assures us, that he was the first person who asserted the identity of Hesperus and Lucifer." πρῶτόν τε Ἕσπερον καὶ Φωσφόρον τὸν αὐτὸν εἰπεῖν, ὥς φησι Παρμενίδης. "It was he who first declared that the Evening and Morning Stars are the same, as Parmenides maintains." "He flourished about the sixty-ninth Olympiad. He appears to have been the first person who discovered that Hesperus and Lucifer were the same star, as Phavorinus records, in the fifth book of his Commentaries. Some, however, attribute this discovery to Pythagoras. And Callimachus asserts that the poem in which this doctrine is promulgated is not his work." καὶ δοκεῖ πρῶτος πεφωρακέναι τὸν αὐτὸν εἶναι Ἕσπερον καὶ Φωσφόρον, ὥς φησι Φαβωρῖνος ἐν πέμπτῳ Ἀπομνημονευμάτων· οἱ δὲ Πυθαγόραν

F57. Diogenes Laertius 8.14 and 9.23: who first said Hesperus is Phosphorus. · tr. original (Greek); Yonge 1853; Hicks (Loeb 1925) · cloud_east_venus-0309

the first to detect the identity of Hesperus, the evening-star, and Phosphorus, the morning-star

The lives of Li Bai

China · 762 and the 9th century

The prefaces of Li Yangbing (762) and Pei Jing on the poet Li Bai, named for the morning star.

驚姜之夕，長庚入夢，故生而名白，以太白字之。世稱太白之精得之矣。 "On the night of the birth-pangs, Changgeng entered the dream; so at birth he was named Bai, and styled Taibai. The world says the essence of Taibai got him; it has it right." (house) 或曰：太白之精下降，故字太白，故賀監號為謫仙，不其然乎！ "Some say: the essence of Taibai came down, hence the style Taibai, hence Secretary He called him the Banished Immortal. Is it not so?" (house)

Frege, On Sense and Reference

Europe, later writers · 1892

The German logician’s essay on meaning, whose best-known example is the evening star and the morning star: one thing, known under two senses.

Es liegt nun nahe, mit einem Zeichen (Namen, Wortverbindung, Schriftzeichen) außer dem Bezeichneten, was die Bedeutung des Zeichens heißen möge, noch das verbunden zu denken, was ich den Sinn des Zeichens nennen möchte, worin die Art des Gegebenseins enthalten ist. Es würde danach in unserem Beispiele [27] zwar die Bedeutung der Ausdrücke „der Schnittpunkt von a und b" und „der Schnittpunkt von b und c" dieselbe sein, aber nicht ihr Sinn. Es würde die Bedeutung von „Abendstern" und „Morgenstern" dieselbe sein, aber nicht der Sinn. "It is natural, then, to think of a sign (a name, a combination of words, a written mark) as carrying, besides what it designates, which may be called the reference of the sign, also what I should like to call the sense of the sign, in which the mode of presentation is contained. On this account the reference of the expressions "the intersection of a and b" and "the intersection of b and c" would be the same, but not their sense. The reference of "evening star" and "morning star" would be the same, but not the sense." (house) denn es ist z.B. der Gedanke des Satzes „der Morgenstern ist ein von der Sonne beleuchteter Körper" verschieden von dem des Satzes „der Abendstern ist ein von der Sonne beleuchteter Körper". Jemand, der nicht wüßte, daß der Abendstern der Morgenstern ist, könnte den einen Gedanken für wahr, den anderen für falsch halten. "for the thought of the sentence "the morning star is a body lit by the sun" is different from that of the sentence "the evening star is a body lit by the sun". Someone who did not know that the evening star is the morning star could hold the one thought true and the other false." (house)

F62. Frege, "Über Sinn und Bedeutung" (1892): one reference, two senses; the man who did not know the evening star is the morning star. · tr. original (German); our rendering · not yet checked against the printed book · cloud_east_venus-0326

Moment 13 of 27 · ✦ not in the one-page story

The star of Inana, al-ʿUzzā and Quetzalcoatl

Sumer knew the star as Inana’s: Enheduana, the first author to sign her name, hails the lady of all the me, light come forth blazing, and a king of Isin praises the Dilbad star at evening. In Arabia the morning star was al-ʿUzzā, whom the Saracens worshipped as Venus and whom the Qurʾān names among the three that are only names. In Mexico Quetzalcoatl, driven from his kingdom, came to the eastern sea and burned himself, and his heart rose as the morning star. The Theosophists of 1913 had the Lords of the Flame come down from Venus in a chariot of fire.

Told by 17 writings from 5 peoples, 26 lines · oldest writing first

Enheduana, The Exaltation of Inana

Mesopotamia · about 2250 BC; later copies

The hymn to Inana by Enheduana, daughter of Sargon and high priestess at Ur, the first author in the world to sign her name.

The Descent of Ishtar

Mesopotamia · 7th century BC copies

The shorter Akkadian telling of the goddess’s descent to the land of no return, from Ashurbanipal’s library at Nineveh and from Aššur, given here in three English versions (King 1899, Gray 1901, Jastrow 1915).

"To the land whence none return, the place of darkness, "Ishtar the daughter of Sin inclined her ear. "The daughter of Sin inclined her ear "To the house of darkness, the seat of the god Irkalla, "To the house from which none who enter come forth again, "To the road whose course returns not, "To the house wherein he who enters is excluded from the light, "To the place where dust is their bread, and mud their food. "They behold not the light, they dwell in darkness, "And are clothed like birds in a garment of feathers; "And over door and bolt the dust is scattered. "If thou openest not thy gate, so that I may not enter, "I will smite the door, I will shatter the bolt, "I will smite the threshold and tear down the doors, "I will raise up the dead, that they may devour the living, "And the dead shall outnumber those that live.' "The First Gate he made her enter, . . . and he took the great crown from off her head. "'Why, O porter, didst thou take the great crown from off my head?' "'Enter, O Lady, for thus are the laws of Allatu.'"

F45. The Descent of Ishtar (King 1899): the opening, the threat at the gate, the first gate. · cloud_east_venus-0253

"Toward the land of No-Return, the region of darkness, Ishtar, the daughter of the Moon-god, directed her attention. The Moon-god's daughter directed her attention Toward the house of darkness, Irkalla's dwelling-place, Toward the house out of which he who enters never comes, Toward the road whose way turns not back, Toward the house where he who enters is deprived of light, A place where dust is their sustenance, their food clay. Light they see not, they sit in darkness. They are clothed, like a bird, with feathered raiment. Over door and bolt is spread the dust." "The seventh gate he caused her to enter, he approached and took off the garment covering the shame of her body. "Why, O porter, hast thou taken off the garment covering the shame of my body?" "Enter, my lady, for such is the custom of Allatu." As soon as Ishtar had descended to the land of No-Return, Allatu saw her and moved angrily toward her. Ishtar took no counsel, she threw herself upon her (Allatu)."

The Qurʾān

Arabia and Islam · 7th century AD

The scripture of Islam; 2:102 tells of what was sent down to the two angels at Babylon, Hārūt and Mārūt. English of Richard Bell.

"What think ye of Allat, and Al Uzza, / and Manah, that other third goddess? / Have ye male children, and God female? / This, therefore, is an unjust partition. / They are no other than empty names, which ye and your fathers have named goddesses. GOD hath not revealed concerning them anything to authorize their worship." "أَفَرَأَيْتُمُ اللَّاتَ وَالْعُزَّىٰ ۝ وَمَنَاةَ الثَّالِثَةَ الْأُخْرَىٰ ... إِنْ هِيَ إِلَّا أَسْمَاءٌ سَمَّيْتُمُوهَا أَنْتُمْ وَآبَاؤُكُمْ مَا أَنْزَلَ اللَّهُ بِهَا مِنْ سُلْطَانٍ" Rodwell: "These are mere names: ye and your fathers named them thus"

"We have sent no apostle, or prophet, before thee, but, when he read, Satan suggested some error in his reading. But GOD shall make void that which Satan hath suggested: then shall GOD confirm his signs; for GOD is knowing and wise." "وَمَا أَرْسَلْنَا مِنْ قَبْلِكَ مِنْ رَسُولٍ وَلَا نَبِيٍّ إِلَّا إِذَا تَمَنَّىٰ أَلْقَى الشَّيْطَانُ فِي أُمْنِيَّتِهِ فَيَنْسَخُ اللَّهُ مَا يُلْقِي الشَّيْطَانُ ثُمَّ يُحْكِمُ اللَّهُ آيَاتِهِ"

al-Ṭabarī, History of the Prophets and Kings

Arabia and Islam · early 10th century

The great history of the Baghdad scholar who died in 923, collecting what the first Muslims said of the beginning.

"And see ye not Lat and Ozza, / And Manat the third besides? / ... the devil suggested an expression of the thoughts which for many a day had possessed his soul; and put into his mouth words of reconciliation and compromise ... / These are the exalted Females, / And verily their Intercession is to be hoped for. / ... In the evening Gabriel visited him; and the Prophet recited the Sura unto him. And Gabriel said, What is this that thou hast done? thou hast repeated before the people words that I never gave unto thee. / ... So the two Satanic verses were in the mouth of every one of the unbelievers ..." Ṭabarī: "ألقى عليه الشيطان كلمتين: تلك الغرانقة العلى، وإن شفاعتهن لترجى" "قال: ما جئتك بهاتين، فقال رسول الله صلى الله عليه وسلم: افتريت على الله، وقلت على الله ما لم يقل" "وكان ذلك الحرفان اللذان ألقى الشيطان على لسان رسوله قد وقعا في فم كل مشرك" (house) "Satan cast upon him two sayings: "Those are the high cranes, and their intercession is surely hoped for." ... [Gabriel] said, "I did not bring you these two." And the Messenger of God said, "I have forged against God, and said of God what He did not say." ... And those two sayings which Satan had cast upon the tongue of His messenger had fallen into the mouth of every idolater."

F72. Muir on al-Wāqidī and al-Ṭabarī, and Ṭabarī's Arabic: the two sentences cast upon the tongue; the cranes. · tr. Muir 1861; original; our rendering · not yet checked against the printed book · cloud_troy_lucifer-0401

The Annals of Cuauhtitlan

Mesoamerica · about 1570

A Nahuatl chronicle of the Valley of Mexico, in the Latin alphabet.

"When Quetzalcoatl, so runs the legend, driven from his kingdom by the artifices of the "magician" Tezcatlipoca, journeyed eastward and came to the seacoast, into the tlillan tlapallan, "the land of the black and red colors", that is, the land of writing or the land of the good example, into the tlatlayan, "the place of burning", he donned his ornaments, the feather ornament (quetzalapanecayotl), and the mask of turquoise mosaic (xiuhxayacatl), as the dead were arrayed in the ornament and mask on the funeral pile, and cremated himself. The ashes immediately flew upward and were metamorphosed into birds of all kinds having brilliant plumage … When the ashes were scattered the heart also flew upward and, reaching heaven, transformed itself into the morning star. "They said that Quetzalcoatl died when the star became visible, and henceforward they called him lord of the dawn (Tlauizcalpan tecutli). They said that when he died he was invisible for four days; they said he wandered in the underworld, and for four days more he was bone (dead, or emaciated?). Not until eight days were past did the great star (the morning star) appear. They said that Quetzalcoatl then ascended the throne as god"." "At the time when the planet (as the evening star) was visible in the sky Quetzalcoatl died (yn yu[h] quitoa yn icuac necico yn mic Quetzalcoatl). And when Quetzalcoatl was dead he was not seen for 4 days; they said that then he dwelt in the underworld, and for 4 more days he was bone (that is, he was emaciated, he was weak); not until 8 days had passed did the great star appear (ye chicueylhuitica yn necico huey citlalli), that is, as the morning star. They said that then Quetzalcoatl ascended the throne as god (yn quitoaya Quetzalcoatl quitoa ya ycuac moteuhtlali)." "This death of Quetzalcoatl is said to have occurred in the year '1 reed' (ce Acatl). Hence the divinity of the morning star was also called Ce Acatl" "When the morning star rose, says the same author, they stopped up the chimneys and smoke vents, so that no harm of any kind should enter the house with its light; but it was sometimes regarded as propitious (according to the time in which it appeared in the east)."

F64. Seler's Anales de Cuauhtitlan: Quetzalcoatl burns himself; the heart becomes the morning star; four days and four days; the dangerous dawn. · tr. Seler 1904 (BAE Bulletin 28) · cloud_east_venus-0337

George Sale, Preliminary Discourse to the Koran

Arabia and Islam · 1734

The English translator’s introduction to his Koran, on the gods of pagan Arabia, among them al-ʿUzzā and the morning star.

the most mighty

F74. Sale's Preliminary Discourse: al-Uzza, " … "; a Venus temple built by Dahac at Sanaa. · tr. Sale 1734 · cloud_troy_lucifer-0414

"Al Uzza, as some affirm, was the idol of the tribes of Koreish and Kenânah, and part of the tribe of Salim: others tell us it was a tree called the Egyptian thorn, or acacia, worshipped by the tribe of Ghatfân ... Khâled Ebn Walîd being sent by Mohammed in the eighth year of the Hejra to destroy this idol, demolished the chapel, and cutting down this tree or image, burnt it: he also slew the priestess, who ran out with her hair dishevelled, and her hands on her head as a suppliant. ... The name of this deity is derived from the root azza, and signifies the most mighty." "... one of which in particular, called Beit Ghomdân, was built in Sanaa, the metropolis of Yaman, by Dahac, to the honour of al Zoharah or the planet Venus, and was demolished by the Khalîf Othman; by whose murder was fulfilled the prophetical inscription set, as is reported, over this temple, viz., "Ghomdân, he who destroyeth thee shall be slain.""

Daniel G. Brinton, American Hero-Myths

Mesoamerica · 1882

The American scholar’s study of the hero-gods of the native Americas, which tells Quetzalcoatl’s death by the eastern sea in a shorter form.

He journeyed eastward until he came to a place where the sky, and land, and water meet together. There his attendants built a funeral pile, and he threw himself into the flames. As his body burned his heart rose to heaven, and after four days became the planet Venus.

Blavatsky, “What’s in a Name?”

The chronologers · 1887

Helena Blavatsky’s essay explaining why her magazine was called Lucifer, the pale morning star before the noon-day sun.

""Lucifer," is the pale morning-star, the precursor of the full blaze of the noon-day sun—the "Eosphoros" of the Greeks. It shines timidly at dawn to gather forces and dazzle the eye after sunset as its own brother 'Hesperos'—the radiant evening star, or the planet Venus." "If one analyzes his rebellion, however, it will be found of no worse nature than an assertion of free-will and independent thought, as if Lucifer had been born in the XIXth century." "Christ, we are told, is the "Morning Star," the divine Lucifer; and Satan the usurpator of the Verbum, the "infernal Lucifer.""

Wellhausen, Remnants of Arab Paganism

Arabia and Islam · 1887

The German orientalist’s study of the old Arabian religion, which identifies al-ʿUzzā with the morning star, worshipped by women on the roofs and boys at dawn.

"Aus den Zeugnissen des syrischen Historikers und des Isaak von Antiochia erhellt also, dass die Uzza gemeint ist, wenn es heisst, die Araber verehren die Venus oder den Morgenstern." (house) "From the testimony of the Syriac historian and of Isaac of Antioch it is therefore clear that Uzza is meant when it is said that the Arabs worship Venus or the morning star. ... [Isaac:] "Boys and girls were sacrificed by the wild Arabs to Kaukabta." ... [the women] go up on the roof and call on Kaukabta, that she may increase their beauty. ... [Nilus, of the Saracens who seized young Theodulus at Sinai:] "For they know no god, neither one of the mind nor one made with hands, but they worship the morning star, and when it rises they slaughter to it the best of the booty ... preferably boys in their bloom, whom they sacrifice on heaped stones at dawn." ... Meanwhile the morning star peeped out; the Saracens were still asleep, and slept on until the sun rose and it was too late for the sacrifice."

Blavatsky, The Secret Doctrine (the Stanzas of Dzyan)

The chronologers · 1888

Helena Blavatsky’s Theosophical work, built on the Stanzas of Dzyan, which she said she translated from a secret book.

"The Beings, or the Being, collectively called Elohim, who first (if ever) pronounced the cruel words, 'Behold, the man is become as one of us, to know good and evil; and now, lest he put forth his hand and take also of the tree of life and eat and live for ever . . . ' must have been indeed the Ilda-baoth, the Demiurge of the Nazarenes, filled with rage and envy against his own creature". And: he "conferred on us spiritual, instead of physical immortality — the latter a kind of static immortality that would have transformed man into an undying 'Wandering Jew.'" (SD II p. 243) "The Fall was the result of man's knowledge, for his 'eyes were opened.' Indeed, he was taught Wisdom and the hidden knowledge by the 'Fallen Angel,' for the latter had become from that day his Manas, Mind and Self-consciousness." And: "Lucifer, or 'Light-Bearer,' is in us: it is our Mind — our tempter and Redeemer, our intelligent liberator and Saviour from pure animalism." (SD II p. 513) "for Lucifer is the Logos in his highest, and the 'Adversary' in his lowest aspect — both of which are reflected in our Ego." (SD II p. 162) "'Lucifer,' is the pale morning-star, the precursor of the full blaze of the noon-day sun—the 'Eosphoros' of the Greeks. It shines timidly at dawn to gather forces and dazzle the eye after sunset as its own brother 'Hesperos'—the radiant evening star, or the planet Venus." (Lucifer I.1, 'What's in a Name?')

F32. Lucifer is the mind: the SD and the magazine named for him. · in the original language · u13-0355

"'Every sin committed on Earth is felt by Usanas-Sukra. The Guru of the Daityas is the Guardian Spirit of the Earth and Men. Every change on Sukra is felt on, and reflected by, the Earth.' Sukra, or Venus, is thus represented as the preceptor of the Daityas, the giants of the Fourth Race, who, in the Hindu allegory, obtained at one time the sovereignty of all the Earth, and defeated the minor gods." (SD II p. 31) "Usanas (Sukra, or Venus), the bosom-friend of Soma and the foe of Brihaspati (Jupiter) the instructor of the gods, whose wife Tara (or Taraka) had been carried away by the Moon, Soma — 'of whom he begat Budha' — took also an active part in this war against 'the gods' and forthwith was degraded into a demon (Asura) deity, and so he remains to this day." (SD II p. 45) "Azazel and his 'host' are simply the Hebrew 'Prometheus,' and ought to be viewed from the same standpoint. The Zohar shows the Ischin chained on the mountain in the desert, allegorically; thus simply alluding to those 'spirits' as being chained to the earth during the cycle of incarnation." (SD II p. 376) "The demi-god robs the gods (the Elohim) of their secret — the mystery of the creative fire. For this sacrilegious attempt he is struck down by kronos and delivered unto Zeus, the father and creator of a mankind which he would wish to have blind intellectually, and animal-like; a personal deity, which will not see man 'like one of us.' Hence Prometheus, 'the fire and light-giver,' is chained on Mount Caucasus and condemned to suffer torture." (SD II p. 414)

E. A. Wallis Budge, on the Egyptian names of Venus

Egypt · early 20th century

The British Museum Egyptologist’s notes on the names of the planet Venus in Egypt, with the Book of the Dead’s Bennu, the morning star of Ra; the ledger does not say which of his books each line comes from.

"5. Venus was called the "star of the ship of the Bennu-Asar," [hieroglyphs] under the XIXth and XXth Dynasties, and "Pi-neter-Tuau," i.e., the "god of the morning," in the Graeco-Roman period. The god of this planet was Osiris. As an evening star Venus was called Sbat uatitha," ""I go in like the Hawk, and I come forth like the Bennu, the Morning Star (i.e., the planet Venus) of Ra" (xiii. 2); "I am the Bennu which is in Heliopolis" (xvii. 27), and the scholion on this passage expressly informs us that the Bennu is Osiris." "Venus as a morning star was identified with him; Benu was self-produced, and the bird appeared each morning at dawn on the Persea Tree in Anu; the Greeks connected it with the Phoenix" "Pi-neter-tuau … Lanzone, 20, the god of the planet Venus; he had a man's head and a hawk's head." "Neter tuaut (Tuaut neter) … P. 611, star of the morning — Venus" "Sba-tcha … Thes. … the planet Venus; other names …" "tcha, tchai … to cross a river in a boat, to make a passage"

F63. Budge: the Egyptian names of Venus; the Bennu that is Osiris; the dictionary entries. · tr. Budge 1904; Budge 1920 · cloud_east_venus-0333

The Theosophical tellings

The chronologers · 1913 and after

Annie Besant and C. W. Leadbeater’s Man: Whence, How and Whither (1913), with the Lords of the Flame coming down from Venus, beside Rudolf Steiner’s lectures.

"Then, with the mighty roar of swift descent from incalculable heights, surrounded by blazing masses of fire which filled the sky with shooting tongues of flame, flashed through the aerial spaces the chariot of the Sons of the Fire, the Lords of the Flame from Venus; it halted, hovering over the ' White Island,' which lay smiling in the bosom of the Gobi Sea". "There He stood, 'the Youth of sixteen summers,' Sanat Kumara, the 'Eternal Virgin-Youth,' the new Ruler of Earth, come to His kingdom, His Pupils, the three Kumaras, with Him, His Helpers around Him; thirty mighty Beings were there". (BL 1913, para. 177) "Oduarpa, enthroned on a pile of corpses, and surrounded by his animal and half-animal guards, was crowned Emperor of the City of the Golden Gates, assuming the desecrated title of ' Divine Ruler' . But his triumph was not of long duration, for Vaivasvata Manu marched against him with a great army ... he himself was shut up in a tower whither he had fled in the rout. The building was fired, and he perished miserably, literally boiled to death within his materialised metallic shell." (BL 1913, para. 238) "With their guidance man had to learn to find the laws of his being. Under Lucifer's leadership he himself had to become as 'one of the gods.'" (Steiner, Cosmic Memory ch. 10, para. 5, Zimmer) "Eine wunderbare, herrliche Sage der Menschheit spricht davon, daß dem Luzifer, als er vom Himmel auf die Erde herunterstürzte, ein Edelstein aus seiner Krone fiel." ... "Es wurde aus dem Stein, der entfiel der Krone Luzifers, der heilige Gral." ('A wonderful, glorious legend of mankind tells that when Lucifer fell from heaven to earth a jewel fell from his crown. ... From the stone that fell from Lucifer's crown came the Holy Grail.', ours) (GA 113, Munich, 23 Aug 1909, para. 13) "daß Christus von einer kosmischen zu einer innermenschlichen Wesenheit, Luzifer von einer innermenschlichen zu einer kosmischen Wesenheit geworden ist" ('that Christ has become, from a cosmic being, a being within man; Lucifer, from a being within man, a cosmic being.', ours) (GA 113, 29 Aug 1909)

F35. The twentieth-century tellings: the landing from Venus, the king who took "Divine Ruler", and Steiner's Lucifer. · tr. original (BL 1913); Zimmer; our rendering (GA 113) · not yet checked against the printed book · u13-0359

Langdon and Jastrow on Ishtar-Venus

Mesopotamia · early 20th century

The notes of two Assyriologists, Stephen Langdon and Morris Jastrow, on Dilbat, the Babylonian name of the planet Venus, and the two offices of Ishtar as morning and evening star.

"16. [ ] ᵈDilbat 16. [ ] is Ishtar-Venus." "Ishtar of Erech is Venus as evening star, the effeminate Venus of Erech, see Tammuz and Ishtar, 54 and 180 n. 4." "Venus as morning star. The Ishtar of Agade was the type of war goddess, see op. cit. Pp. 100; hence Venus as morning star is sometimes called the Bow Star, KUGLER, Sternkunde II 108." "su-ud-da-äm = nūr šamê, a title of Innini as Venus, SBH. 98, 1." "Ishtar is here identified with the planet Venus and assigned to a place therefore in the heavens. As such she is called "the daughter of Sin," the moon-god."

↑ the moments

Moment 14 of 27 · ✦ not in the one-page story

The rebel’s side, told again

From the seventeenth century the poets let the fallen speak. Milton’s Satan says the mind is its own place and that it is better to reign in hell than serve in heaven, and Vondel’s Lucifer refuses the decree that sets man above the angels. Byron’s Lucifer has a victor but no superior, while Madách’s has lived from all eternity. Blake makes the Devil the original archangel, and Carducci hails Satan as the one for whom Adonis and Astarte lived.

Told by 7 writings from 1 people, 11 lines · oldest writing first

Vondel, Lucifer

Europe, later writers · 1654

The Dutch tragedy of the angel who will not accept the decree that sets man above the angels, with God’s morning-star standing at its height.

"Ye speedy Spirits, stay our chariot now, / God's Morning-star in its full zenith stands; / Its height is reached; and lo! the moment comes / When Lucifer must set before this star, / This double star that rises from below / And seeks the way above, to tarnish Heaven / With earthly glow. No more should ye adorn / Proud Lucifer's apparel with glittering crowns, / Nor gild his forehead with the glorious dawn / Of morning-star, to which Archangels kneel." "Though Spirits may seem pre-eminent, above / All other beings, yet God hath decreed, / Even from eternity, that man shall high / Exalted be, even o'er the Angel world;" "... besides, / To be the first prince in some lower court / Is better than within the Blessed Light / To be the second, or even less." "Thou erring Morning-star, oh! spare thyself!" Beside it the dossier sets Iblīs, Qur'an 7:12 in Sale: "I am more excellent than he: thou hast created me of fire, and hast created him of clay"

F64. Vondel, Lucifer (1654): the double star rising from below; the decree that sets man above angels; the refusal. · tr. Van Noppen 1898; Sale 1734 · cloud_troy_lucifer-0376

Milton, Paradise Lost

Europe, later writers · 1667

John Milton’s English epic of the war in heaven and the fall.

"But the narrative of Hephaestus binding Here his mother, or how on another occasion Zeus sent him flying for taking her part when she was being beaten, and all the battles of the gods in Homer—these tales must not be admitted into our State, whether they are supposed to have an allegorical meaning or not." "Men called him Mulciber; and how he fell From Heaven they fabled, thrown by angry Jove Sheer o'er the crystal battlements: from morn To noon he fell, from noon to dewy eve, A summer's day, and with the setting sun Dropt from the zenith, like a falling star, On Lemnos, th' Aegaean isle. Thus they relate, Erring; for he with this rebellious rout Fell long before"

F53. Republic 378d and Paradise Lost 1.740-748: the fall of the smith censored by Plato and restored by Milton as a fallen angel's. · tr. Jowett 1892; Milton 1674 · cloud_greece-0329

"The mind is its own place, and in itself / Can make a Heaven of Hell, a Hell of Heaven." "Here we may reign secure; and, in my choice, / To reign is worth ambition, though in Hell: / Better to reign in Hell than serve in Heaven." "Though on their names in Heavenly records now / Be no memorial, blotted out and rased / By their rebellion from the Books of Life. / Nor had they yet among the sons of Eve / Got them new names, till, wandering o'er the earth, / Through God's high sufferance for the trial of man, / By falsities and lies the greatest part / Of mankind they corrupted to forsake / God their Creator ... / Then were they known to men by various names, / And various idols through the heathen world."

F61. Paradise Lost 1.254-263 and 1.361-375: the reign; the names blotted out and new names got among men. · in the original language · cloud_troy_lucifer-0365

"First, Moloch, horrid king, besmeared with blood / Of human sacrifice, and parents' tears ... / With these came they who, from the bordering flood / Of old Euphrates to the brook that parts / Egypt from Syrian ground, had general names / Of Baalim and Ashtaroth—those male, / These feminine. For Spirits, when they please, / Can either sex assume, or both ... / With these in troop / Came Astoreth, whom the Phoenicians called / Astarte, queen of heaven, with crescent horns ... / After these appeared / A crew who, under names of old renown— / Osiris, Isis, Orus, and their train— / With monstrous shapes and sorceries abused / Fanatic Egypt ..." "Th' Ionian gods—of Javan's issue held / Gods, yet confessed later than Heaven and Earth, / Their boasted parents;—Titan, Heaven's first-born, / With his enormous brood, and birthright seized / By younger Saturn: he from mightier Jove, / His own and Rhea's son, like measure found; / So Jove usurping reigned. These, first in Crete / And Ida known, thence on the snowy top / Of cold Olympus ruled the middle air, / Their highest heaven; or on the Delphian cliff, / Or in Dodona, and through all the bounds / Of Doric land; or who with Saturn old / Fled over Adria to th' Hesperian fields, / And o'er the Celtic roamed the utmost Isles."

Blake, The Marriage of Heaven and Hell

Europe, later writers · about 1790–1793

William Blake’s prophetic book, which says Milton was of the Devil’s party without knowing it.

"And the original Archangel or possessor of the command of the heavenly host is called the Devil, or Satan, and his children are called Sin and Death." "But in the book of Job, Milton's Messiah is called Satan." "For this history has been adopted by both parties." "It indeed appeared to Reason as if desire was cast out, but the Devil's account is, that the Messiah fell, and formed a heaven of what he stole from the abyss." "Note.--The reason Milton wrote in fetters when he wrote of Angels and God, and at liberty when of Devils and Hell, is because he was a true poet, and of the Devil's party without knowing it." "I was in a printing-house in Hell, and saw the method in which knowledge is transmitted from generation to generation." "The Giants who formed this world into its sensual existence and now seem to live in it in chains are in truth the causes of its life and the sources of all activity, but the chains are the cunning of weak and tame minds, which have power to resist energy ..."

F66. Blake, The Marriage of Heaven and Hell, plates 5-6, 15-16: the Devil's party; the Messiah who fell; the printing-house in Hell; the giants in chains. · in the original language · cloud_troy_lucifer-0381

Byron, Cain

Europe, later writers · 1821

Lord Byron’s verse drama, in which Lucifer leads Cain through the abyss of space.

"Souls who dare use their immortality-- / Souls who dare look the Omnipotent tyrant in / His everlasting face, and tell him that / His evil is not good!" "I have a Victor--true; but no superior. / Homage he has from all--but none from me ... / He as a conqueror will call the conquered / Evil; but what will be the Good he gives?" "Think and endure,--and form an inner world / In your own bosom--where the outward fails;" "Cain. And what are they? / Lucifer. That which / Thou shalt be. / Cain. But what were they? / Lucifer. Living, high, / Intelligent, good, great, and glorious things, / As much superior unto all thy sire / Adam could e'er have been in Eden ... / Cain. And wherefore did it fall? / Lucifer. Ask him who fells. / Cain. But how? / Lucifer. By a most crushing and inexorable / Destruction and disorder of the elements, / Which struck a world to chaos, as a chaos / Subsiding has struck out a world: such things, / Though rare in time, are frequent in eternity.--" Byron's note: "the author has partly adopted in this poem the notion of Cuvier, that the world had been destroyed several times before the creation of man ... The assertion of Lucifer, that the pre-Adamite world was also peopled by rational beings much more intelligent than man ... is, of course, a poetical fiction to help him to make out his case." II.i.151-153: "To what was before thee! / The phantasm of the world; of which thy world / Is but the wreck."

F65. Byron, Cain (1821): souls who dare; a Victor but no superior; the pre-Adamites and the one who fells. · in the original language · cloud_troy_lucifer-0379

Madách, The Tragedy of Man

Europe, later writers · 1861

Imre Madách’s Hungarian dramatic poem, in which Lucifer leads Adam through the ages of history.

"But I have lived, from all eternity." "Didst thou not ever feel a rift, a void; / A hindrance to thy mind, which still compelled / Thee to create, despite thyself? The name / Of this gigantic rift, is — Lucifer, / The spirit of Negation from all time;" "Together we created; I lay claim / Now to my part." "How niggardly thou portionest; but thou / Art a great Lord. A foot of ground, for me, / Will serve; for whereso'er Negation's foot / Shall tread, 'twill bring destruction to thy world." The angels' chorus (p. 18): "How modest shineth yonder star, / Soon to be called the star of love; / The earthly race, with tender hand / She guards, a solace from above." "From our exalted standpoint, this is vain, / When from this height, we look on things below, / As first the charming details lose their glow, / Their force and grandeur fade, through vision's trick, / And what remains, is cold arithmetic." "Voice of the Earth Spirit: ... I need not tell my name; thou knowest me, / The spirit of the Earth; the boundary / To all my power is this. Return, I say, / And thou wilt live;" "Lucifer (Laughing.) ... Why have I striven so long with man, of mire / And sunshine molded? He is too dwarfed for wisdom, / Yet too great for blindness." "The Lord. ... And thou too Lucifer, thou art a link / In my great cosmos; so pursue thy work, / For thy cold wisdom and absurd negation / Will be the leaven that causes fermentation;" The last line: "I've told thee, man, strive and trust!"

F67. Madách, The Tragedy of Man (1861), Scenes I, XIII, XV: the rift named Lucifer; the claim to a share; the earth from space; the Earth Spirit; the leaven. · tr. Loew 1908 · cloud_troy_lucifer-0385

Anatole France, The Revolt of the Angels

Europe, later writers · 1914

Anatole France’s novel of angels who rebel again in modern Paris.

"At length he raised his darkened visage. Now he was Satan, greater than Lucifer." "In their gratitude the people of Greece and of Asia consecrated to us stones, trees, shadowy woods; offered us victims, and sang us hymns; in fact we became gods in their sight, and they called us Horus, Isis, Astarte, Zeus, Cybele, Demeter, and Triptolemus. Satan was worshipped under the names of Evan, Dionysus, Iacchus, and Lenæus." "And lo! Ialdabaoth was now contemplating the Earth and, seeing it sunk in wickedness and suffering, he began to foster thoughts of kindliness in his heart. On a sudden he rose up, and beating the ether with his mighty arms, as though with oars, he hastened thither to instruct and to console mankind." ""Comrades," said the great archangel, "no--we will not conquer the heavens. Enough to have the power. War engenders war, and victory defeat." ""God, conquered, will become Satan; Satan, conquering, will become God. May the fates spare me this terrible lot; I love the Hell which formed my genius. I love the Earth where I have done some good ..." ""Nectaire, you fought with me before the birth of the world. We were conquered because we failed to understand that Victory is a Spirit, and that it is in ourselves and in ourselves alone that we must attack and destroy Ialdabaoth."" Inside the dream, Satan gives Michael back his sword: "It is Lucifer who yields it to you."

Moment 15 of 27

He said he was Viracocha

Some claimants were found out: in Peru Viracocha warned before he left that people would come saying they were Viracocha, and a servant came back to preach that he was the creator, until the people saw it was false. The Maya Seven Macaw puffed himself up as the sun before there was a sun, but he was not truly the sun, and what is left of him is a mirror. Simon the magician carried a woman from Tyre about as Helen, his first thought and mother of all, and had himself worshipped in the likeness of Jupiter. The Mandaeans warn of a Christ clothed in fire who says, I am God, and is not Hibil-Ziwa.

Euripides, Helen

Greece · 412 BC

Euripides’ play in which the real Helen waits in Egypt while the Greeks and Trojans fight ten years for an image made of air.

and one of the Achaeans, that had but a single ship, did light a blazing beacon on sea-girt Euboea, and destroy full many of them, wrecking them on the rocks of Caphareus and the shores that front the Aegean main, by the treacherous gleam he kindled; when thou, O Menelaus, from the very day of thy start, didst drift to harbourless hills, far from thy country before the breath of the storm, bearing on thy ship a prize that was no prize, but a phantom made by Hera out of cloud for the Danai to struggle over. What mortal claims, by searching to the utmost limit, to have found out the nature of God, or of his opposite, or of that which comes between, seeing as he doth this world of man tossed to and fro by waves of contradiction and strange vicissitudes?

Irenaeus, Against Heresies

Christian, Gnostic and Hermetic · about AD 180

The bishop of Lyon’s refutation of the Gnostic teachers, which quotes their myths.

"Having redeemed from slavery at Tyre, a city of Phœnicia, a certain woman named Helena, he was in the habit of carrying her about with him, declaring that this woman was the first conception of his mind, the mother of all, by whom, in the beginning, he conceived in his mind [the thought] of forming angels and archangels. For this Ennœa leaping forth from him, and comprehending the will of her father, descended to the lower regions [of space], and generated angels and powers, by whom also he declared this world was formed. But after she had produced them, she was detained by them through motives of jealousy, because they were unwilling to be looked upon as the progeny of any other being. ... She suffered all kinds of contumely from them, so that she could not return upwards to her father, but was even shut up in a human body, and for ages passed in succession from one female body to another, as from vessel to vessel. She was, for example, in that Helen on whose account the Trojan War was undertaken; for whose sake also Stesichorus was struck blind, because he had cursed her in his verses, but afterwards, repenting and writing what are called palinodes, in which he sang her praise, he was restored to sight. Thus she, passing from body to body, and suffering insults in every one of them, at last became a common prostitute; and she it was that was meant by the lost sheep" (1.23.2)

F21. Helen of Troy as the First Thought, "from vessel to vessel". Text: Irenaeus on Simon of Samaria; Hippolytus, Refutation of All Heresies (c. 222-235). Passage: Irenaeus 1.23.2 and 1.23.4; Hippolytus Ref. 6.14. Edition: Ante-Nicene Fathers English. Rights … · u10-0427

Moment 16 of 27

The gods of the Arabs, given back

Arabia’s record is cut in stone. Esarhaddon of Assyria says he refurbished six captured gods of the Arabs, Atar-samayin among them, and gave them back with the might of Aššur and his own name inscribed on them. Over the Arabs he set the lady Tabūʾa, who was raised in his father’s palace, and a rebel was put in neck stocks at the gate. Herodotus describes the Arabian pledge, sworn by their gods Orotalt and Alilat.

Esarhaddon’s Nineveh inscription

Mesopotamia · about 670 BC

The Assyrian king’s account of his reign, which tells how he refurbished the captured gods of the Arabs, inscribed them with the might of Aššur and his own name, and set over the Arabs a woman raised in his father’s palace. Edition of Erle Leichty (RINAP 4).

(As for) the city Adumutu, the fortress of the Arabs, which Sennacherib, king of Assyria, (my) father, who engendered me, conquered and whose goods, possessions, (and) gods, together with Apkallatu, the queen of the Arabs, (iv 5) he plundered and brought to Assyria — Hazael, the king of the Arabs, came to Nineveh, my capital city, with his heavy audience gift and kissed my feet. He implored me to give (back) his gods, and I had pity on him.

F39. Esarhaddon 1 iv 1-9: Adumutu, Apkallatu the queen, Hazael at Nineveh. · tr. Leichty (ORACC RINAP 4) · atlantis_arabia-0078

(iv 10) I refurbished the gods Atar-samayin, Dāya, Nuḫāya, Ruldāwu, Abirillu, (and) Atar-qurumâ, the gods of the Arabs, and I inscribed the might of the god Aššur, my lord, and (an inscription) written in my name on them and gave (them) back to him. (iv 15) I placed the lady Tabūʾa, who was raised in the palace of my father, as ruler over them and returned her to her land with her gods.

F40. Esarhaddon 1 iv 10-16: six gods refurbished and inscribed; Tabūʾa made ruler. · tr. Leichty (ORACC RINAP 4) · atlantis_arabia-0081

(iv 17) I added sixty-five camels (and) ten donkeys to the previous tribute and imposed (it) on him. Hazael died and I placed Iataʾ, his son, (iv 20) on his throne. I added ten minas of gold, one thousand choice stones, fifty camels, (and) one hundred bags of aromatics to the tribute of his father and imposed (it) on him. Later, Uabu, to exercise kingship, incited all of the Arabs to rebel against Iataʾ.

F41. Esarhaddon 1 iv 17-24: the tribute; Hazael dies; Iataʾ enthroned; Uabu rebels. · tr. Leichty (ORACC RINAP 4) · atlantis_arabia-0082

(iv 25) I, Esarhaddon, king of Assyria, king of the four quarters, who loves loyalty and abhors treachery, sent my battle troops to the aid of Iataʾ, and they trampled all of the Arabs, threw Uabu, together with the soldiers who were with him, into fetters, and brought (them) to me. I placed them in neck stocks and tied them to the side of my gate.

F42. Esarhaddon 1 iv 25-31: the rebel in neck stocks at the gate. · tr. Leichty (ORACC RINAP 4) · atlantis_arabia-0083

Herodotus, Histories, book 3

Greece · about 430 BC

Book 3 of Herodotus, on the Persian king Cambyses, with the long-lived Ethiopians at the edge of the world who scorn his gifts.

"A man different from those who desire to give the pledges to one another, standing in the midst between the two, cuts with a sharp stone the inner parts of the hands, along by the thumbs, of those who are giving the pledges to one another, and then he takes a thread from the cloak of each one and smears with the blood seven stones laid in the midst between them; and as he does this he calls upon Dionysos and Urania." "Of gods they believe in Dionysos and Urania alone" "Now they call Dionysos Orotalt and Urania they call Alilat."

Moment 17 of 27

Obodas the king, whom they make a god

At Petra and in the Negev the Nabataeans made a king a god. Stephanus of Byzantium, quoting Uranius, records the place where Obodas the king, whom they make a god, lies buried. A Petra inscription remembers the banqueting-society of Obodat the god, and at the spring of ʿAyn ʿAvdat people swore as Obodat lives. At Miletus Syllaios, brother of the king, made a dedication for the life of King Obodas.

Told by 4 writings from 2 peoples, 9 lines · oldest writing first

↑ the moments

Moment 18 of 27

Who holds the gods holds the water

In Yemen the gods and the water went together. A Sabaean king besieged a rival city by order of ʿAthtar, seized it for Almaqah and for Saba, cut its land off from the water and made it build a temple of Almaqah in its midst. Centuries later the Christian ruler Abraha opened his inscription on the Maʾrib dam with the Merciful, his Messiah and the Holy Spirit, then told how the dam and its sluices broke and the tribes were summoned to seal the breaches. Another Sabaean ruler, Yathaʿamar Watar, made a dedication when he brought his patron god Aranyadaʿ home.

Told by 3 writings from 1 people, 10 lines · oldest writing first

A Sabaean dedication (Louvre AO 31929)

Arabia and Islam · early first millennium BC

An inscription of Yathaʿamar Watar, ruler of Sabaʾ in south Arabia, who brought the god Aranyadaʿ home and allied him with Almaqah.

"Yathaʿamar Watar, son of Yakrubmalik mukarrib of Sabaʾ dedicated to Aranyadaʿ the Patron when he brought Aranyadaʿ back from Kamna to Nashshan" (lines 1-2) "because Nashshan had maintained the alliance of Almaqah and Aranyadaʿ and Yathaʿamar and Malikwaqah (Mlkwqh) and with Sabaʾ and with Nashshan, founded on the god and the patron and the pact and the alliance." (lines 4-5)

Moment 19 of 27 · ✦ not in the one-page story

Descended from Troy

Peoples claimed descent through the house of Troy. Snorri’s Prologue puts Troy at the centre of the earth and makes Thor a grandson of Priam, whose people went north and were taken for gods, and Geoffrey sends Brutus the Trojan to an island held by giants. The Persians traced their name to Perseus’s son Perses, and the Ethiopian royal house in Heliodorus counted the Sun, Perseus, Andromeda and Memnon among its ancestors. Rome fetched the Mother of the Gods from Phrygia when the Sibylline books called for her, the goddess who had almost followed Aeneas’s ships, and the Irish put Fénius Farsaid where Isidore had put Magog, as father of the Scythians and the Goths.

Told by 23 writings from 9 peoples, 40 lines · oldest writing first

Virgil, Aeneid

Rome · about 19 BC

Virgil’s Latin epic of Aeneas the Trojan, who comes to Italy where Saturn once hid.

"Ye long-suffering sons of Dardanus, the land which bare you first from your parent stock shall welcome you back to her fruitful bosom. Seek out your ancient mother. There the house of Aeneas shall lord it over all lands, even his children's children and their race that shall be born of them." "Antiquam exquirite matrem" (3.96) "be not unaware that the Latins are Saturn's race, righteous not by bond or laws, but self-controlled of their own free will and by the custom of their ancient god. And in truth I remember, though time has dimmed the tale, that Auruncan elders told how that in this land sprang Dardanus, and hence passed to the towns of Phrygian Ida and Thracian Samos, that men now call Samothrace. 'Twas hence, from the Tuscan home of Corythus, he came, and now the golden palace of the starry sky admits him to a throne, and with his altars he increases the number of the gods." "Saturni gentem, haud vinclo nec legibus aequam" (7.203)

F7. Aeneid 3.94-98 and 7.202-211: the ancient mother; Saturn's race; Dardanus born in Italy and throned among the stars. · tr. Fairclough 1916; Fairclough 1918 · cloud_troy_lucifer-0211

Pausanias, Description of Greece

Greece · about AD 160–180

A Greek traveller’s guide to the sanctuaries, with the Phrygian story that the body of Attis would neither rot nor decay.

I saw a statue, still sitting, which gave out a sound. The many call it Memnon, who they say from Aethiopia overran Egypt and as far as Susa. The Thebans, however, say that it is a statue, not of Memnon, but of a native named Phamenoph ... This statue was broken in two by Cambyses ... every day at the rising of the sun it makes a noise, and the sound one could best liken to that of a harp or lyre when a string has been broken.

The coins of Apamea Kibotos

Anatolia and the Levant · about AD 202–253

Bronze coins of the Phrygian city that called itself the Ark, showing Noah’s chest on the waves and the satyr Marsyas on his rock.

Herodian, with Livy

Greece · 3rd century AD; Livy about 25 BC

Herodian’s Greek history of the emperors says the Mother of the Gods’ image fell from heaven; Livy tells how it was fetched from Pessinus to Rome in 204 BC.

The state was at this time suddenly occupied with a question of a religious nature, in consequence of the discovery of a prediction in the Sibylline books, which had been inspected on account of there having been so many showers of stones this year. It ran thus: Whensoever a foreign enemy should bring war into the land of Italy, he may be driven out of Italy and conquered, if the Idaean Mother should be brought from Pessinus to Rome.

Nennius, History of the Britons

Ireland and Britain · about 829

A Latin history of Britain that gives Brutus two pedigrees, one through Aeneas and one through Noah’s son Japheth back to Adam.

"After the deluge, the three sons of Noah severally occupied three different parts of the earth: Shem extended his borders into Asia, Ham into Africa, and Japheth into Europe. The first man that dwelt in Europe was Alanus, with his three sons, Hisicion, Armenon, and Neugio. Hisicion had four sons, Francus, Romanus, Alamanus, and Brutus. ... Joham of Japheth, Japheth of Noah, Noah of Lamech, Lamech of Mathusalem, Mathusalem of Enoch, Enoch of Jared, Jared of Malalehel, Malalehel of Cainan, Cainan of Enos, Enos of Seth, Seth of Adam, and Adam was formed by the living God. ..." "§ 18. The Britons were thus called from Brutus: Brutus was the son of Hisicion, Hisicion was the son of Alanus, Alanus was the son of Rhea Silvia, Rhea Silvia was the daughter of Numa Pompilius, Numa was the son of Ascanius, Ascanius of Eneas, Eneas of Anchises, Anchises of Troius, Troius of Dardanus," On Japheth's sons: "from the second, Magog, the Scythi and Gothi". Giles's footnote: "This genealogy is different in almost all the MSS."

Geoffrey of Monmouth, History of the Kings of Britain

Ireland and Britain · about 1136

The Latin history that brings Brutus, Aeneas’s descendant, to an island held by giants.

"Brutus! there lies beyond the Gallic bounds / An island which the western sea surrounds. / By giants once possess'd; now few remain / To bar thy entrance, or obstruct thy reign. / To reach that happy shore thy sails employ; / There fate decrees to raise a second Troy, / And found an empire in thy royal line. / Which time shall ne'er destroy, nor bounds confine." Geoffrey's Latin as Giles prints it: "Brute! sub occasum solis trans Gallica regna / Insula in oceano est undique clausa mari: / Insula in oceano est habitata gigantibus olim, / Nunc deserta quidem, gentibus apta tuis. / Hanc pete, namque tibi sedes erit illa perennis: / Sic fiet natis altera Troja tuis."

F31. Geoffrey of Monmouth I.11: Diana's oracle, an island once held by giants, a second Troy. · tr. Giles 1842 (verse Thompson's); original (Latin) · cloud_troy_lucifer-0267

itaque ut apud Graecos Dianam, eamque Luciferam

Text: Geoffrey of Monmouth, British History, J. A. Giles (1842; "English verse is Thompson's, revised by Giles"). VERIFIED. Guardrail: "Geoffrey is twelfth-century pseudo-history; the English verse of Diana's oracle is Thompson's and Giles's, the elegiacs are … · in the original language · cloud_troy_lucifer-0270

The island was then called Albion, and was inhabited by none but a few giants. ... They therefore passed through all the provinces, forced the giants to fly into the caves of the mountains, and divided the country among them according to the directions of their commander. ... At last Brutus called the island after his own name Britain, and his companions Britons; for by these means he desired to perpetuate the memory of his name. From whence afterwards the language of the nation, which at first bore the name of Trojan, or rough Greek, was called British. ... Among the rest was one detestable monster, named Goemagot, in stature twelve cubits, and of such prodigious strength, that at one shake he pulled up an oak, as if it had been a hazel wand. ... Corineus, highly enraged, roused up his whole strength, and snatching him upon his shoulders, ran with him, as fast as the weight would allow him, to the next shore, and there getting upon the top of a high rock, hurled down the savage monster into the sea; where falling on the sides of craggy rocks, he was torn to pieces, and coloured the waves with his blood. The place where he fell, taking its name from the giant's fall, is called Lam Goemagot, that is, Goemagot's Leap, to this day.

F27. Prose Edda, Prologue III-IV: Troy at the earth's centre, " … "; Tror whom we call Thor; Sibil whom we call Sif; the Æsir go north and are taken for gods. · tr. Brodeur 1916 · cloud_troy_lucifer-0255

"Near the earth's centre was made that goodliest of homes and haunts that ever have been, which is called Troy, even that which we call Turkland. ... One king among them was called Munon or Mennon; and he was wedded to the daughter of the High King Priam, her who was called Troan; they had a child named Tror, whom we call Thor. ... In the northern half of his kingdom he found the prophetess that is called Sibil, whom we call Sif, and wedded her. The lineage of Sif I cannot tell; she was fairest of all women, and her hair was like gold." "Odin had second sight, and his wife also; and from their foreknowledge he found that his name should be exalted in the northern part of the world and glorified above the fame of all other kings. Therefore, he made ready to journey out of Turkland ... And wherever they went over the lands of the earth, many glorious things were spoken of them, so that they were held more like gods than men." "The Æsir took wives of the land for themselves, and some also for their sons; and these kindreds became many in number, so that throughout Saxland, and thence all over the region of the north, they spread out until their tongue, even the speech of the men of Asia, was the native tongue over all these lands."

Moment 20 of 27

The losers’ book travelled

The losers’ side of the story survives because its books travelled. The Book of Giants, which tells the fallen angels’ and their sons’ side, went from the Dead Sea caves to the followers of Mani; a Parthian letter says a brother has taken it with him and another copy was made at Merv. A Chinese list of Mani’s books calls it the Sūtra of the Mighty Warriors, and the church banned the book about the giant Ogias who fought a dragon after the flood. Augustine, nine years a hearer among the Manichaeans, reports their teaching that particles of God stay bound in the fruit until the elect release them by eating.

Told by 6 writings from 3 peoples, 12 lines · oldest writing first

↑ the moments

Moment 21 of 27

Its witnesses hide their faces

Some books were written down in hiding: the Popol Vuh says the original book exists, but its witnesses and those who ponder it hide their faces. An old man of Cholula, a hundred years of age, told the friar Durán to take ink and paper, and Sahagún gathered the oldest men of Tlatelolco to set down what they knew. The Kebra Nagast tells how Zion was carried off from Jerusalem, so that the daughters of Zion were rejected and the daughters of Ethiopia honoured, and says it went untranslated under the Zagwe kings. The Mande griots call themselves the keepers of the oaths the ancestors swore.

This is the very chant which was sung by Puou, the High Priest of our ancient worship, to Captain Cook whom they had surnamed Lono ... For it was prophesied by priests at the time of the death of Ka-I-i-mamao that he, Lono, would return anew from the sea in a Spanish man-of-war or Auwaalalua.

The refrain of the night eras: "So the gods may enter, but not man." The gods born late: "Hanau ka po Akua" (line 601). The mixing: "Hanau La'ila'i he wahine / Hanau Ki'i he kane / Hanau Kane he akua" (lines 612-614); "Kupakupa the settler, the woman who sat … · tr. Liliuokalani · u10-0496

↑ the moments

Moment 22 of 27 · ✦ not in the one-page story

The book that came down from heaven

Many archives say their oldest book came from the gods. Edfu’s builders followed a book that descended from the sky north of Memphis, and the prince Setne went looking for the book Thoth wrote with his own hand. Berossus has Xisuthrus bury all writings at Sippar before the flood, while the Book of Sothis claims inscriptions traced in sacred language by Thoth and translated after it. Euhemerus said he found a golden column on which Zeus wrote his own deeds, and Blavatsky said an archaic manuscript of palm leaves lay before her eyes.

Told by 24 writings from 11 peoples, 39 lines · oldest writing first

Atra-ḫasīs

Mesopotamia · copied about 1635 BC

The Babylonian poem of the Exceedingly Wise, in Akkadian, which tells the gods’ strike, the making of man and the flood.

Oldest copyLondon, British Museum, BM 78941, with tablets in New Haven and Geneva; one scribe at Sippar signed and dated each (H5)

"The land had become great; the people had multiplied. / The land like a bull had become satiated. / [In] their assemblage God was absent. / ... heard their clamor. / He said to the great gods (?) / Those observing the clamor of men." (Text A, col. I 2-7, in Clay's "Selection from the early version") Colophon: "The second tablet (of the series) "When / God, man." / Its total is 439 (lines) / By the hand of Azag-dAya, the junior / scribe. / Month Shebet, day 28th / of the year when Ammi-zaduga, the king, / built the city Dûr-Ammi-zaduga / at the mouth / of the Euphrates (11th year)." Clay's introduction: "It was copied from a still earlier inscription by a junior scribe named Azag-Aya, on the 28th day of Shebet, in the 11th year of Ammi-zaduga (1966 B.C.) ... The original from which the scribe copied had already been injured in the 12th line, which is indicated by the word hibis " broken.""

F10. Clay 1922, the Morgan tablet (Old Babylonian Atra-ḫasīs, Tablet II): the noise, and the dated colophon. Public domain. · u1r-0089

Erra and Išum

Mesopotamia · first millennium BC

A Babylonian poem of the plague-god Erra’s rampage and the Seven who march at his side.

"The singer who chants (it) shall not die in pestilence." "The scribe who masters it shall be spared in an enemy land and honored in his own." "The house in which this tablet is placed ... though Erra be angry and the Seven be slaughtering ... The sword of pestilence shall not approach it, safety abides upon it." The god "revealed it in the nighttime" to the poet, who "omitted nothing at all. Nor one line did he add to it." (V 43-44)

F4. Erra V: the poem that protects the house it hangs in. · tr. Foster · u12-0492

Bīt mēseri

Mesopotamia · first millennium BC copies

A Babylonian incantation series, House of the Enclosure, that lists the seven sages before the flood and four after it.

"Nungalpiriĝgaldim, the sage of Enme(r)kar, who had Ištar descend from heaven into the @i{ayyakku-}sanctuary, / Piriĝgalnungal, who was created in Kiš, who enraged Adad in heaven and (thus) caused rain and green growth to be absent from the land for three years, / Piriĝgalabzu, who was created in Utab, who hung his seal on a goat-fish and (thereby) enraged Ea in the Subterranean Ocean so that a fuller struck him down with the seal on his throat, / fourth is Lu-Nanna, two-thirds sage, who let a dragon out of the E-Ninkiʾaĝnuna, the temple of Ištar (built) by Šulgi. / The four sages of human descent whom Ea, the lord, made perfect with great wisdom!" Sumerian of 297: "nun-gal-piriĝ-gal-dim₂ abgal en-me-kar₂ dinnana ša₃ e₂-an-na-ke₄ an-ta e₁₁-de₃".

F22. Bīt mēseri 297-306: the four after the Flood, the ones remembered for trouble, and "of human descent". · tr. eBL · u1r-0114

Berossus, History of Babylon

Mesopotamia · about 290 BC

A priest of Babylon who wrote his country’s history in Greek. The book is lost; its lines survive in extracts made by Alexander Polyhistor, Apollodorus, Eusebius and Syncellus, printed in Cory’s Ancient Fragments of 1832.

the beginnings, middles, and ends of all writings

F3. Berossus, the buried writings. Xisouthros is commanded to bury " … " at Sippar. Babyloniaca (c. 290 BC), via Alexander Polyhistor and Abydenus in Syncellus (ed. Mosshammer); Concordance I.7. No note given. · breshears-0108

in the city of the Sun at Sippara

Text: The Flood story ("Eridu Genesis"), Sumerian, one Old Babylonian tablet from Nippur (CBS 10673, per Unit 1). ETCSL 1.7.4. The five cities are the King List's five (F1), in the same order. Zimbir (Sippar) belongs to the sun god Utu; Berossus's Xisuthrus … · tr. Cory 1832 · u1r-0079

"After the death of Ardates, his son Xisuthrus reigned eighteen sari. In his time happened a great Deluge; the history of which is thus described. The Deity, Cronus, appeared to him in a vision, and warned him that upon the fifteenth day of the month Desius there would be a flood, by which mankind would be destroyed. He therefore enjoined him to write a history of the beginning, procedure, and conclusion of all things; and to bury it in the city of the Sun at Sippara; and to build a vessel, and take with him into it his friends and relations; and to convey on board every thing necessary to sustain life, together with all the different animals, both birds and quadrupeds, and trust himself fearlessly to the deep. Having asked the Deity, whither he was to sail? he was answered, "To the Gods:" upon which he offered up a prayer for the good of mankind." "Xisuthrus then paid his adoration to the earth: and having constructed an altar, offered sacrifices to the gods, and, with those who had come out of the vessel with him, disappeared. They, who remained within, finding that their companions did not return, quitted the vessel with many lamentations, and called continually on the name of Xisuthrus. Him they saw no more; but they could distinguish his voice in the air, and could hear him admonish them to pay due regard to religion; and likewise informed them that it was upon account of his piety that he was translated to live with the gods; that his wife and daughter, and the pilot, had obtained the same honour. To this he added, that they should return to Babylonia; and, as it was ordained, search for the writings at Sippara, which they were to make known to all mankind: moreover that the place, wherein they then were, was the land of Armenia." "The vessel being thus stranded in Armenia, some part of it yet remains in the Corcyræan mountains of Armenia; and the people scrape off the bitumen, with which it had been outwardly coated, and make use of it by way of an alexipharmic and amulet. And when they returned to Babylon, and had found the writings at Sippara, they built cities, and erected temples: and Babylon was thus inhabited again."

F32. Berossus: Xisuthrus, the buried writings at Sippara, the translation to the gods. Public domain, Cory 1832. · u1r-0138

Diodorus of Sicily, Library of History

Greece · 1st century BC

A Greek universal history; book 3 tells of the Ethiopians and of the Atlantians by the western ocean.

Now Euhemerus, who was a friend of King Cassander and was required by him to perform certain affairs of state and to make great journeys abroad, says that he travelled southward as far as the ocean; for setting sail from Arabia the Blest he voyaged through the ocean for a considerable number of days and was carried to the shore of some islands in the sea, one of which bore the name of Panchaea. ... There is also on the island, situated upon an exceedingly high hill, a sanctuary of Zeus Triphylius, which was established by him during the time when he was king of all the inhabited world and was still in the company of men. And in this temple there is a stele of gold on which is inscribed in summary, in the writing employed by the Panchaeans, the deeds of Uranus and Cronus and Zeus.

Virgil, Aeneid

Rome · about 19 BC

Virgil’s Latin epic of Aeneas the Trojan, who comes to Italy where Saturn once hid.

the god, lo! the god

F9. Aeneid 3.443-452, 6.74-76 and 6.35-51: the Sibyl's leaves; " … ". · tr. Fairclough 1916 · cloud_troy_lucifer-0214

"shalt look on an inspired prophetess, who deep in a rocky cave sings the Fates and entrusts to leaves signs and symbols. Whatever verses the maid has traced on leaves she arranges in order and stores away in the cave. These remain unmoved in their places and quit not their rank; but when at the turn of the hinge a light breeze has stirred them, and the open door scattered the tender foliage, never does she thereafter care to catch them, as they flutter in the rocky cave, nor to recover their places, nor to unite the verses; uncounselled, men depart, and loathe the Sibyl's seat." "fata canit, foliisque notas et nomina mandat." (3.444) "Only trust not thy verses to leaves, lest they fly in disorder, the sport of rushing winds; chant them thyself, I pray." (6.74-76) "with him the priestess of Phoebus and Trivia, Deiphobe, daughter of Glaucus ... They had come to the threshold, when the maiden cries: "'Tis time to ask the oracles; the god, lo! the god!" As thus she spake before the doors, suddenly nor countenance nor colour was the same, nor stayed her tresses braided; but her bosom heaves, her heart swells with wild frenzy, and she is taller to behold, nor has her voice a mortal ring, since now she feels the nearer breath of deity." "deus, ecce, deus!" (6.46)

Taautus was the first who thought of the invention of letters, and began the writing of records: and he laid the foundation, as it were, of his history, by beginning with him, whom the Egyptians called Thoyth, and the Alexandrians Thoth, translated by the Greeks into Hermes.'

F31. PE 1.9.24: Taautus the first to think of letters; Thoyth, Thoth, Hermes. · tr. Gifford 1903 · atlantis_arabia-0070

"'But before this the god Tauthus imitated the features of the gods who were his companions, Kronos, and Dagon, and the rest, and gave form to the sacred characters of the letters. He also devised for Kronos as insignia of royalty four eyes in front and behind . . . but two of them quietly closed, and upon his shoulders four wings, two as spread for flying, and two as folded." "'And when Kronos came into the South country he gave all Egypt to the god Tauthus, that it might be his royal dwelling-place. And these things, he says, were recorded first by Suduc's seven sons the Cabeiri, and their eighth brother Asclepius, as the god Tauthus commanded them."

Blavatsky, The Secret Doctrine (the Stanzas of Dzyan)

The chronologers · 1888

Helena Blavatsky’s Theosophical work, built on the Stanzas of Dzyan, which she said she translated from a secret book.

"An Archaic Manuscript — a collection of palm leaves made impermeable to water, fire, and air, by some specific unknown process — is before the writer's eye. On the first page is an immaculate white disk within a dull black ground." (SD I p. 1) "It is more than probable that the book will be regarded by a large section of the public as a romance of the wildest kind; for who has ever even heard of the book of Dzyan? The writer, therefore, is fully prepared to take all the responsibility for what is contained in this work, and even to face the charge of having invented the whole of it." (SD I p. viii, Preface, dated London, October 1888) "Not only this latter and the Siphrah Dzeniouta but even the Sepher Jezirah, ... the book of Shu-king, China's primitive Bible, the sacred volumes of the Egyptian Thoth-Hermes, the Puranas in India, and the Chaldean Book of Numbers and the Pentateuch itself, are all derived from that one small parent volume." (SD I p. xliii) "Only forty-nine Slokas out of several hundred are here given. Not every verse is translated verbatim." (SD II p. 15 n.)

F30. The Book of Dzyan: the source-book asserted, given in part, and owned as possibly invented. · in the original language · u13-0350

↑ the moments

Moment 23 of 27 · ✦ not in the one-page story

The historian’s own sight

Some writers weighed the claims and said so. Philo of Byblos says Hesiod and the cyclic poets made up their theogonies and with them defeated the truth, and Herodotus marks where his own sight, judgement and inquiry end and the Egyptians’ report begins. The priest of Saïs tells Solon the Greeks are all children, and Stesichorus recants: that story is not true. Movses of Armenia, weighing the fables, decides that much of them is true, and Sima Qian, of the clan that once cut heaven from earth, set the most refined of the old words at the head of his history.

Told by 25 writings from 9 peoples, 46 lines · oldest writing first

Prometheus Bound

Greece · 5th century BC

A tragedy staged at Athens under the name of Aeschylus, spoken by the Titan’s son nailed to a rock at the edge of the world.

Full oft my mother Themis, or Earth (one form she hath but many names), had foretold to me the way in which the future was fated to come to pass—how that it was not by brute strength and not through violence, but by guile that those who should gain the upper hand were destined to prevail.

F26. Prometheus Bound 211-215: Themis, or Earth: one form, many names. · tr. Smyth 1922 · cloud_greece-0230

Herodotus, Histories, book 2

Greece · about 430 BC

The Greek historian’s inquiry; book 2 is his Egypt, from what the priests told him.

"Within the enclosure of Proteus there is a temple called the temple of the "foreign Aphrodite," which temple I conjecture to be one of Helen the daughter of Tyndareus, not only because I have heard the tale how Helen dwelt with Proteus, but also especially because it is called by the name of the "foreign Aphrodite," for the other temples of Aphrodite which there are have none of them the addition of the word "foreign" to the name." "In truth however they lacked the power to give Helen back; and the Hellenes did not believe them, though they spoke the truth; because, as I declare my opinion, the divine power was purposing to cause them utterly to perish, and so make it evident to men that for great wrongs great also are the chastisements which come from the gods." "When they occur singly they are mischievous, so as even to sink the vessels, and if they strike on the lower part of the keel, setting them on fire. When there are two of them they are considered auspicious, and are thought to predict a prosperous voyage, as it is said that they drive away that dreadful and terrific meteor named Helena. On this account their efficacy is ascribed to Castor and Pollux, and they are invoked as gods."

F61. Herodotus 2.112 and 2.120, Pliny 2.101: Helen as the "foreign Aphrodite" among the Tyrians of Memphis; the Trojans who told the truth and were not believed; the meteor named Helena. · tr. Macaulay 1890; Bostock and Riley 1855 · cloud_greece-0359

Round about this enclosure dwell Phenicians of Tyre, and this whole region is called the Camp of the Tyrians.

Plato, Phaedrus

Greece · 4th century BC

Plato’s dialogue on love and speech, which quotes the poet Stesichorus taking back his story of Helen.

"Wherefore I must have a purgation. And I bethink me of an ancient purgation of mythological error which was devised, not by Homer, for he never had the wit to discover why he was blind, but by Stesichorus, who was a philosopher and knew the reason why; and therefore, when he lost his eyes, for that was the penalty which was inflicted upon him for reviling the lovely Helen, he at once purged himself. And the purgation was a recantation, which began thus,-- 'False is that word of mine--the truth is that thou didst not embark in ships, nor ever go to the walls of Troy;' and when he had completed his poem, which is called 'the recantation,' immediately his sight returned to him." "they are fought about as Stesichorus says that the Greeks fought about the shadow of Helen at Troy in ignorance of the truth."

F52. Phaedrus 243a-b and Republic 586c: the palinode of Stesichorus; the shadow fought over in ignorance. · tr. Jowett 1892 · cloud_greece-0326

That story is not true.
